In a little over a week, Is-raeli ground forces have fought their way into northern Gaza’s urban areas with aspeed that has surprised even their own commanders. But complex stages of the war still lie ahead. As they move closer to the center of Gaza City, Israeli troops are encountering dug-in Hamas fighters in larger numbers who attack and then pull back or disappear under-ground, slowing the Israeli ad-vance, soldiers and current and former officials say. “The further we went in, the harder the battle became,” said an Israeli captain who led a reservist tank unit into northern Gaza in the initial days of the invasion and fought into the heart of the city. One of Israel’s objectives— Al-Shifa hospital in Gaza City’s western perimeter—could also be its most challenging. By Eric Wallerstein ,

Rebecca Feng 

and David Uberti 

TOKYO—For a quarter-century, Japan tried every trick in the central-banking book to boost prices and jolt its troubled economy back to life. The cure turned out to be pan-demic and war. Higher energy and food costs triggered by the Ukraine war, coupled with Covid-era sup-ply-chain snags, are bringing a surprise end to the long, bleak era of Japanification. Busi-nesses are increasing prices significantly, and wages have begun inching up. “It’s like a misfortune that turned into a blessing,” said Etsuro Honda, an economic adviser to the late Prime Minister Shinzo Abe. “Thanks to these disasters, the defla-tionary mindset has disappeared.” For decades, Japanification has been short-hand for the economic curse of falling prices and stagnant growth. Declining prices might sound like a good thing to Western consum-ers hammered by inflation, but policy makers

Walt Disney Co. is examin-ing whether its future should include fewer television net-works. After Chief Executive Bob Iger said last summer that some of Disney’s TV networks, which include ABC, FX and National Geographic, might not be core to the company, executives set out to deter-mine which channels have long-term value and which are expendable. As part of the review, the company has explored poten-tial sales and discussed put-ting some of its TV networks into A+E Networks, its joint venture with Hearst, accord-ing to people familiar with the matter. Disney’s traditional TV net-works were once cash cows but are suffering from viewer-ship declines as streaming re-places the cable bundle. # U.S. NEWS 

In a U.S. News graphic on Friday showing how the new federal income-tax brackets work for a married couple fil-ing jointly with adjusted gross income of $300,000, the 22% bracket starts at $94,300 and the 24% bracket starts at $201,050. The graphic incor-rectly gave those figures as $71,100 and $106,750, respec-tively. 

A Mansion article on Fri-day about a home in the Brentwood neighborhood of Los Angeles was accompanied by a headline that incorrectly described the neighborhood as near Los Angeles. 

An article about Alex Rod-riguez in November’s WSJ. Magazine incorrectly referred to Rodriguez’s half-brother, who was in the U.S. Air Force, as his stepbrother. 

Kylie Jenner and her sister previously had a mass-market clothing line, Kendall & Kylie, that was produced under a li-cense. An article in Novem-ber’s WSJ. Magazine about Jenner incorrectly said the clothing line is still active. 

A photo collage with an ar-ticle in November’s WSJ. Mag-azine about Jerry Lorenzo and the Fear of God fashion brand incorrectly identified as au-thentic a photo of a counter-feit Essentials hooded sweat-shirt and a counterfeit hooded sweatshirt purporting to be from Fear of God’s collabora-tion with Zegna.

# CORRECTIONS  AMPLIFICATIONS 

ELECTIONS 

Fentanyl, Threats Mailed to Offices 

While workers were count-ing ballots for primary elec-tions in August, the elections office in King County, Washing-ton, received a suspicious en-velope that turned out to con-tain trace amounts of fentanyl. It happened again this week, and not just in Wash-ington state, where the office was processing ballots from the general election and had to be evacuated. Election of-fices in at least five states were sent threatening mail, some containing the poten-tially deadly drug, authorities say. Election workers across the country have been be-sieged by threats, harass-ment and intimidation since the 2020 election. King County was one of at least four counties in Wash-ington with election offices that were evacuated this week after they received en-velopes containing suspicious powders—including two that field-tested positive for fen-tanyl—while workers were processing ballots from Tues-day’s election. Authorities say suspicious letters also were sent to elec-tion offices in four other states: Georgia, Nevada, Cali-fornia and Oregon—with some being intercepted be-fore they were delivered. 

—Associated Press 

VIRGINIA 

Windmill-Blade Factory Canceled 

A European company has canceled plans to build blades for offshore wind turbines in coastal Virginia, the latest sign of struggle within the U.S.’s nascent industry. Siemens Gamesa con-firmed the cancellation Fri-day. The proposed $200 mil-lion factory at the Port of Virginia in Portsmouth would have created more than 300 jobs and aided the state in its aspirations to become a hub for offshore wind proj-ects amid the nation’s efforts to tackle climate change. Inflation, higher interest rates and supply-chain issues have cut into the profitabil-ity—and even viability—of some offshore wind projects in the U.S. Siemens Gamesa said Fri-day that it had called off building the Virginia factory because “development mile-stones…could not be met.” It didn’t elaborate. The nixed plans, however, won’t impact the construc-tion of Dominion Energy’s enormous wind farm off the coast of Virginia Beach. Those turbines will come from Siemens Gamesa facili-ties in Europe. Dominion said its 176-tur-bine project would be the largest offshore wind farm under development in the U.S. 

—Associated Press

THE NUMBERS | By Josh Zumbrun 

# The Challenges With Tallying the Dead 

# The war in Gaza has cast a spotlight on the grim science of counting casualties in the conflict. The number of deaths from the war between Hamas and Is-rael has become both a focus of concern and a source of controversy, especially when compared with other wars, such as Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. Researchers who have studied civilian and military casualties offer this impor-tant caveat: Casualty counts are often wrong and cannot be easily compared from one conflict to another. “True estimates at the time of conflict are usually inaccu-rate,” said Dr. Amir Khorram-Manesh, a lecturer in surgery and disaster medicine at Swe-den’s University of Gothen-burg. “It is a component of the ‘fog of war’ where the un-certainty of every aspect of battle is confusing, unknown and often inaccurate.” The reasons for caution are many. The belligerents aren’t data agencies; they are often motivated to play down civilian deaths at their hands while playing up those at their enemy’s. They avoid disclosing military losses to protect morale, but they highlight their own civilian losses for sympathy. In a review of 66 casualty studies, Khorram-Manesh and co-authors conclude that the methods used in cross-border wars to tally wounded and dead are “in-sufficient for modern war-fare.” They add that “inter-national organizations responsible for collecting such data…face difficulties to access the conflict scene, re-sulting in underreported, un-reliable or no-reported data.” The Health Ministry in the Hamas-controlled Gaza Strip says more than 11,000 have died, most of them women and children. It doesn’t dis-tinguish between civilians and militants. The Biden ad-ministration has said it doesn’t trust the Gaza Health Ministry to provide accurate data. Nonetheless, a U.S. official has said the overall death toll is likely higher than currently cited. The United Nations doesn’t currently have figures of its own. Previously, the U.N.’s Of-fice for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs has gone into the field to review and verify casualties from the conflict. In 2014, the Palestin-ian Health Ministry said 2,310 people died during a 50-day war between Israel and Hamas. The U.N.’s verifi-cation process resulted in a similar estimate: 2,251. But it isn’t yet possible to compare the Palestinian numbers with other conflicts because estimates of those conflicts’ death tolls might not be accurate. Many na-tions traditionally focused only on military casualties, and estimating civilian casu-alties can take years, said Taylor Seybolt, an associate professor at the University of Pittsburgh who coedited the book “Counting Civilian Casualties.” 

# Consider the war in Ukraine. The U.N.’s Of-fice of the High Com-missioner for Human Rights estimated 9,806 civilians have died since Russia in-vaded on Feb. 24 last year. One might be tempted to in-fer that more people have therefore died in Gaza than Ukraine. But that isn’t right. First, Gaza’s figures in-clude both military and civil-ian deaths. Second, there are huge omissions in the U.N.’s Ukraine estimate, about which it is transparent. It doesn’t include deaths in some of the war’s most im-portant urban battlegrounds: Mariupol, Popasna, Lysy-chansk and Severodonetsk, which are now all controlled by Russia. Detailed verification can take years. Last year, the U.N. completed an estimate of civilian deaths in Syria’s civil war from 2011 to 2021: 306,000. In some cases, detailed verification might never come. The civil war in Ethio-pia’s Tigray region is esti-mated by researchers at Bel-gium’s Ghent University to have killed between 311,000 and 808,000 as of the end of 2022. But while Gaza has captured the world’s atten-tion, Ethiopia vigorously avoided it, banning travel to the region, blacking out me-dia, shutting down telephone and internet service, and ex-pelling humanitarian work-ers. Because comprehensive lists of the dead are impossi-ble, researchers sometimes estimate the percentage of a population that died in a war by asking people after-ward how many siblings they had and whether any died in the war. On average this method finds nearly three times as many victims as verified lists do, according to a 2008 study in BMJ, for-merly known as the British Medical Journal, which com-pared data from both meth-ods over 50 years. Yet numbers can be wrong in both directions. It was common to claim 200,000 to 300,000 deaths (including civilian and mili-tary) in Bosnia while Yugo-slavia was breaking up. Eventually, a team of re-searchers in Sarajevo went through and attempted to document their names. They identified 97,207 victims, ac-cording to the book “Sex, Drugs, and Body Counts: The Politics of Numbers in Global Crime and Conflict.” 

# ‘I t has long been popu-lar in some circles to argue that a drive for statistical accuracy in con fl ict environments is un-necessary, misguided, or even dangerous. What is most germane are not facts on the ground, so the argu-ment goes, but rather the aims and objectives,” wrote co-author Kelly Greenhill, a Tufts University professor. But Greenhill recounts how this can go awry. During the Kosovo War in 1998-99, U.S. officials said it was pos-sible that as many as 100,000 Kosovars were miss-ing and possibly murdered. Media reports widely claimed 10,000 Kosovo citi-zens had been massacred by Serbian soldiers. An anony-mous U.S. official told media the numbers were “conser-vative in the extreme.” Ulti-mately, the North Atlantic Treaty Organization inter-vened to stop the conflict. Years later, however, when Serbian leader Slobo-dan Milosevic was on trial for war crimes, the Interna-tional Criminal Tribunal de-termined the number of bod-ies in mass graves was 2,788. The discrepancy between the earlier claims and eventual bodies found was used to discredit a key witness against Milosevic (who died before the trial’s conclusion). Even the lower figure was a grisly statistic. In an epi-sode of the television show “M*A*S*H,” Alan Alda’s character, Hawkeye, tells a priest that war is worse than hell: “There are no innocent bystanders in hell. War is chock-full of them.” By this token, any deaths of inno-cent civilians are too many. Counting them incorrectly can distract from that.

BY BEN KESLING 

Federal Bureau of Investi-gation agents seized New York City Mayor Eric Adams’s cell-phones and iPad as a part of a public-corruption probe into his political campaign, accord-ing to people familiar with the matter. In a statement Friday, alawyer for the campaign said Adams’s devices were taken from him after he attended an event Monday night. “The Mayor immediately complied with the FBI’s re-quest and provided them with electronic devices,” said Boyd Johnson, Adams’s campaign attorney. “The mayor has not been accused of any wrongdo-ing and continues to cooperate with the investigation.” The seizure came about a week after federal agents searched the Brooklyn home of Brianna Suggs, a fundraiser for his campaign, indicating that the federal investigation is intensifying. After the campaign learned of the federal investigation, “it was discovered that an indi-vidual had recently acted im-properly,” Johnson said. The behavior was reported to in-vestigators, he said, without elaborating on the individual. Some of the electronic de-vices were returned to Adams, a campaign spokesman said. The campaign expected the WASHINGTON—The AR-15 was Jared Golden’s weapon of choice for self-defense. He knew how lethal the gun was, and how easy to use, from his days serving as a Marine, and he kept one in his home in Lewiston, Maine. He was con-fident it would keep him safe. Then last month, a gunman used a weapon much like that one to kill 18 people and wound more than a dozen oth-ers, not more than a five-min-ute jog from the Democratic U.S. representative’s home. The next day, he came out in support of a ban on assault-style weapons. “I have opposed efforts to ban deadly weapons of war,” Golden said at a news confer-ence in Lewiston. “The time has now come for me to take responsibility for this failure, which is why I now call on the United States Congress to ban assault rifles.” It was a rare moment in away in his home, he said. But many mass shootings happen in public places such as the Lewiston bowling alley and bar where the Oct. 25 shoot-ings took place. To truly be ready to stop another shoot-ing or defend himself against a similarly-armed assailant, he would need to carry the weapon everywhere. “Am I going to start carry-ing an AR-15 slung over my shoulder when I go to the gro-cery store, when I go to a res-taurant?” Golden said he asked himself. Even if a well-trained and well-intentioned person such as himself carried a rifle at all times, he said, the odds were remote of being at the right place at the right time with the right angle to take down an active shooter. 

# U.S. NEWS 

politics: Politicians don’t often publicly state they have changed their minds on a sub-ject, especially one as polariz-ing as gun ownership. Perhaps more unusual, Golden’s state-ment came with an apology. “To the people of Lewiston, my constituents throughout the second district, to the families who lost loved ones, and to those who have been harmed, I ask for forgiveness and support as I seek to put an end to these terrible shoot-ings,” Golden said. At a recent interview in his Washington office, which has pictures of himself deployed to Afghanistan as a Marine, holding his government-issued version of the AR-15, Golden spoke of his change. “I really think of gun own-ership as about personal de-fense, and I believe that the Second Amendment is rooted in self-defense,” he said. “I re-ally believe that any law-abid-ing and competent citizen should have fairly easy access to firearms.” But Golden said he found himself “posing what I viewed as some pretty simple ques-tions to myself” the day after the shooting. He kept his rifle locked The three-term congress-man, 41 years old, who repre-sents a largely rural district that twice backed former President Donald Trump, said he was still a strong supporter of gun rights but that assault-style weapons had no place outside of warfare. The final question that he asked himself that day after the shooting, he said, was, “What do we do from here? And what responsibilities do I have as a leader of the com-munity?” Golden said he had long been skeptical of the govern-ment stepping in to decide who should and shouldn’t have weapons. He sees yellow-and red-flag laws—which al-low authorities to seize weap-ons from people who pose a danger to themselves or oth-ers—as often messy and inef-fective. He argues that Second Amendment rights are jeopar-dized more by layering on complex barriers to firearm ownership than by banning assault-style weapons. His answer was to advocate restricting the most lethal types of weapons to everyone. He decided that limiting AR-15s and similar weapons was simpler and cleaner than trying to figure out who should and should not have such weapons. Major gun advocacy groups haven’t spoken out about Golden’s recent announcement. When asked how his change of position might af-fect his chances at re-election, he said he didn’t know. “And that is not my focus anyways,” he said. “And it shouldn’t be.”

Jared Golden seeks ban on assault rifles while still supporting the right to own guns 

Rep. Jared Golden (D., Maine) says assault-style weapons have no place outside of warfare.

rest of the items also would be returned, the spokesman said. Adams, a former NYPD offi-cer, said Friday: “As a former member of law enforcement, I expect all members of my staff to follow the law and fully cooperate with any sort of investigation—and I will continue to do exactly that. I have nothing to hide.” A spokeswoman for the FBI and a spokesman for the Man-hattan U.S. attorney’s office declined to comment. The New York Times re-ported earlier Friday that Ad-ams’s electronic devices were seized, and previously re-ported that a warrant for the Suggs home search indicated authorities were investigating whether Turkish nationals il-legally donated to Adams’s campaign using straw donors. Federal election laws pro-hibit foreign nationals from making contributions in U.S. elections. It also is illegal to make a donation in another individual’s name. The Wall Street Journal hasn’t independently verified the contents of the Suggs search warrant. Facing questions from re-porters after the Suggs home search, Adams said Wednesday that he has repeatedly traveled to Turkey in part to attract people and businesses back to the city, which he noted has a large Turkish population. The mayor said he once spoke to the Turkish president at a nonprofit dinner at some point when he was Brooklyn borough president. He held that post before becoming mayor, from 2014 to 2021. “He greeted me, said hello. We exchanged pleasantries,” Adams recalled. “I said hello. And that was the extent of our conversation.” Adams, a Democrat, won election in 2021, beating out a crowded field of contenders. His campaign has raised $2.5 million donations for his re-election bid in 2025. Suggs worked on his 2021 campaign and has been doing fundraising for his re-election. Adams said Wednesday that he was notified by his campaign team of the raid on Suggs’s home, which prompted him to return to New York from Washington, D.C., where he had planned to meet with White House offi-cials. Adams said he didn’t speak to her that day. “I did not want to give any appearance of interference,” he said, later adding that he has retained the private law firm WilmerHale, and that the lawyers there have been in touch with the agencies han-dling the inquiry.

Most voters, however, will have several choices beyond Trump and Biden—a fact that is not reflected in many public opinion polls. When polls do offer a larger slate of options, Biden often loses the most support to those additional candidates or to “undecided” sta-tus, giving Trump an edge. For Democrats, that prospect took on new urgency Thursday as Sen. Joe Manchin, a Democrat from West Virginia, renewed specu-lation that he would try to run for president That is why many Democrats fear the effect that third parties and independent candidates could have on the president. Liberal intellectual Cornel West and Robert F. Kennedy Jr., known for his antivaccine advocacy, have said they would run as independents. To appear on any state’s ballot, they will need to meet requirements the state sets, which usually requires a signature-gathering effort. Polling suggests that one candidate, Ken-Recent history gives Biden a reason to fear third parties: In 2016, they drew an unusually large share of votes in swing states that Hillary Clinton unexpectedly lost. Voters that year held a largely negative view of both Trump and Clinton, the Democratic nominee. An unusually large share rejected The third parties are gathering signatures and taking other steps now to gain ballot access, as are independents West and Kennedy. The potential of these candidates to become a factor in the election won’t be known until states decide which ones have met the requirements to appear on the ballot. to trick voters into freezing in place a legal framework that conceals the amendment’s po-tentially sweeping legal ef-fects,” Moody wrote in a legal brief filed last month. In Nevada, abortion-rights groups this fall filed a petition to try to place a constitutional amendment strengthening protections for abortion on the ballot. Under state law, it would also need to pass in two elections, in 2024 and 2026. Voters so far have been most motivated in states where there was a real threat of abortion restrictions. While California voters passed an abortion-rights amendment last year, the issue didn’t ap-pear to have a galvanizing ef-fect or spillover impact in a state where abortion access al-ready was robust. The same dynamic could play out in New York. Support-ers say that referendum would make laws restricting abor-tions unconstitutional. State Sen. Liz Krueger, a Manhattan Democrat who sponsored the amendment, said she purpose-fully chose to place the refer-endum on the ballot in 2024. “Voters in New York state are going to the ballot box both for this constitutional amendment but also against people who don’t support these rights,” she said. Iona University political sci-ence professor Jeanne Shee-han Zaino said other voter concerns likely would be more salient. “In New York, while you can scare people on cer-tain things regarding their right to choose and what’s happening elsewhere, they’re not going to be as fearful of that as they are on issues of crime, security and things like inflation,” she said.

cal and advocacy officer for the American Civil Liberties Union. Advocacy groups are pin-ning many of their ballot-ini-tiative hopes on Arizona. That state has some nearly ideal conditions for abortion-rights groups: restrictive abortion laws on the books, a sympa-thetic governor and attorney general and a relatively straightforward process for getting a measure on the bal-lot. The state also has an elec-torate receptive to messages about personal freedom. “When you can put it in the context of freedoms, then you can build big winning coali-tions,” said Tory Gavito, presi-dent of Way to Win, a non-profit that helps get Democratic candidates elected. Once a GOP bastion, Arizona has become apolitical battle-ground, with former Presi-dent Donald Trump losing the state in 2020 after win-ning it four years earlier. The state will also have com-petitive races for the U.S. Senate, House and the state legislature next year. In Florida, abortion-rights groups say they are on track with more than half of the number of signatures they need ahead of a Feb. 1 dead-line. State Attorney General Ashley Moody, a Republican, has filed a challenge to the ini-tiative with the Florida Su-preme Court, arguing its lan-guage is vague and confusing. Abortion proponents “have proposed an amendment to the Florida Constitution using a misleading ballot summary are basically looking for ahome—as Joe Manchin is look-ing for a home,” said Philip Levine, a former Miami Beach, Fla., mayor who backs the group. Manchin didn’t address his future Friday during an ap-pearance in Athens, Ga., at a University of Georgia sympo-sium honoring the late Sen. Johnny Isakson (R., Ga.). But Manchin spoke of their biparti-san work, themes that could form the basis of a future cam-paign. He noted that after the Jan. 6, 2021, attack on the U.S. Capitol, an evenly-divided Sen-ate was able to come together to produce “one of the most pro-ductive times of Congress in his-tory.” D e m o c ra t s have warned that No Labels could help elect Trump and act as a spoiler to Biden’s re-elec-tion chances. Former House Speaker Nancy Pelosi (D., Calif.) said during a breakfast last week held by Third Way, a centrist group that has opposed No Labels’ ef-forts, that No Labels “is peril-ous to our democracy. I said that completely without any hesitation.” Pelosi has warned that No Labels might prevent any pres-idential candidate from secur-ing the necessary 270 electoral votes and would throw the election to the House of Repre-sentatives, giving each state delegation one vote for presi-dent and electing Trump. No Labels has pushed back against those spoiler sugges-tions, and said that putting a Republican candidate atop the ticket might be more advanta-geous to winning the 2024 campaign. Ryan Clancy, the chief strat-egist for No Labels, said in a briefing with reporters this week that polling conducted during the summer in the top eight battleground states found that a ticket led by a Republi-can had a stronger path to vic-tory and pulled more votes from Trump’s column. But he said their process remains fluid. “We certainly don’t have any standard we’re laying down yet about who’s going to be atop the ticket. But we’ll be doing more polling and re-search over time and that was certainly an interesting data point we saw over the sum-mer,” he said. But gaining ballot access could serve as a major hurdle for Manchin or any other poten-tial third-party candidate. Many states require the collection of thousands of signatures to get on the ballot, including signa-tures from individual counties and congressional districts. Clancy said the timeline for No Labels to field a candidate remains March or April 2024 but it would only pursue the campaign if it sees a clear path-way to victory. But he reiterated that polling shows broad num-bers of Americans interested in alternatives to Biden and Trump and expressing open-ness to a third-party candidate.

WASHINGTON—No Labels, the moderate political group, has been exploring the launch of a third-party presidential ticket next spring. A potential No. 1 draft pick just signaled he might be available. West Virginia Sen. Joe Man-chin’s decision not to seek re-election to the Senate next year has intensified questions about whether he might seek the presidency, either as an inde-pendent candidate or through No Labels’ effort. Manchin, a deal-making Democratic senator, has been associated with No Labels for more than a decade. The cen-trist group isn’t required to disclose its donors because it is organized as a nonprofit rather than a traditional political party. It has been laying the groundwork to field an alterna-tive presidential ticket if the 2024 campaign features a re-match of President Biden and former President Donald Trump, adding to fears among Democrats of a third-party spoiler candidate. The 76-year-old West Vir-ginia senator, in announcing his decision, did little to quell speculation about any national ambitions, saying he would be “traveling the country and speaking out to see if there is an interest in creating a move-ment to mobilize the middle and bring Americans together.” People close to Manchin said no decisions had been made on any national travel or a possi-ble presidential campaign. The B-21 Raider took its first test flight on Friday, moving the futuristic war-plane closer to becoming the nation’s next nuclear weap-ons stealth bomber. The Raider flew in Palm-dale, Calif., where it has been under testing and develop-ment by Northrop Grumman. The Air Force is planning to build 100 of the warplanes, which have a flying wing shape much like their prede-cessor the B-2 Spirit but will incorporate advanced materi-als, propulsion and stealth technology to make them more survivable in a future conflict. The plane is planned to be produced in variants 

Treasury market in the U.S. by Bank of New York Mellon, which sits in the middle of the transactions. The bank re-sorted to manually clearing trades. That meant clients whose trades are routed through ICBC were in limbo, unable to determine which positions were still open or which trades have closed. Some analysts conflated the hack on ICBC with a poorly re-ceived sale of 30-year Trea-surys by the U.S. govern-ment on Thurs-day, worrying that Chinese buyers who use ICBC’s platform were unable to participate. ICBC is an important in-termediary for cash-reserve managers in China, according to Ed Al-Hussainy, an analyst at Columbia Threadneedle In-vestments. But it is rare for Chinese buyers to be a major source of demand in long-term U.S. bond auctions. “That was the case 10 or 15 years ago, not so much today,” he said. The hack hit the repo mar-ket, where banks and other fi-nancial institutions exchange trillions of dollars tied to Treasurys mostly overnight, much of it moving through the Depository Trust & Clearing Corp. More than $62 billion of U.S. Treasurys failed to deliver Thursday, DTCC data shows, meaning either sellers didn’t send securities or buyers didn’t receive them in time to settle a trade. scourge in recent years. Hack-ers in previous incidents often locked up companies’ com-puter systems and demanded millions of dollars in payment to restore them. Bankers and U.S. officials have long feared an incident that could disrupt the plumb-ing that enables trading and record-keeping in Treasury markets. The attack shows that wrongdoers aren’t just threatening to steal money, but hold up for ransom the fi-nancial system’s flows of data and information. “A boundary has been bro-ken. We haven’t seen some-thing like this involving alarge bank before,” said Mar-cus Murray, the founder of Truesec, a cybersecurity com-pany. Treasury Secretary Janet Yellen said officials are work-ing with the Chinese govern-ment, the Federal Bureau of Investigation and the Cyberse-curity and Infrastructure Se-curity Agency. “Very good progress has been made, we’ve not seen an impact on the Treasury mar-ket,” Yellen said during apress conference in San Fran-cisco, where she met with her Chinese counterpart. The ransomware victim on Wednesday was ICBC Finan-cial Services, a New York-based entity of the gigantic ICBC. The unit is a clearing-house, which means it ensures brokers’ trades and transac-tions go through. In ransomware incidents, hackers infiltrate companies’ computer systems and inject malware intended to lock up or disable key processes. Cy-bersecurity analysts say at-tackers have demanded sums reaching into eight figures in return for decryption keys that help victims restore their systems—only sometimes suc-cessfully. ICBC was forced to discon-nect and isolate some of its systems after the attack, and it was unplugged from the

ICBC said Friday it was able to clear all trades involving U.S. Treasurys that were exe-cuted on Wednesday, as well as repo financing that took place on Thursday. BNY Mel-lon and DTCC were waiting for assurances ICBC was safe before reconnecting it to the system, which could take until next week, leaving some trades still unclear, market participants said. Clients who could were us-ing other brokers and moving trades away from ICBC, the people said. To accommodate incoming trades on ICBC’s be-half, BNY Mellon requested several extensions to repo-market hours from the Fed on Thursday. ICBC is a relatively minor player in the Treasury mar-kets, and the hack did little to halt trading for U.S. bonds. More than 358,000 Treasurys, worth roughly $822 billion, changed hands on Thursday. That was more trades than any of the previous days that week, according to Financial Industry Regulatory Authority data. The Securities and Ex-change Commission has pushed to force more of the trading tied to Treasurys to go through DTCC’s Fixed Income Clearing Corpo-ration, also known as FICC. SEC Chair Gary Gensler has said moving trading to the FICC platform would boost market resiliency by lessening the risk of incidents among smaller clearinghouses. Wall Street firms have argued such measures would do more harm than good, discouraging new participants due to in-creased costs, complexity and compliance. A DTCC representative said it protected its systems after being notified of the cyberat-tack on ICBC, which remains an active member of FICC. DTCC’s services weren’t im-pacted and remain fully opera-tional, the representative said. They are working with ICBC to determine when it is safe to resume electronic communica-tions with them.

> DAVID SWANSON/REUTERS

groups have called for limits on what is taught in classrooms or offered in libraries and paren-tal notification of students’ gender transitions, among other changes. Their success sparked a re-sponse this election season by progressive groups and candi-dates who criticized library re-strictions as book bans and de-manded protections for gay, lesbian and transgender stu-dents. Those messages ap-peared to resonate in some communities on Election Day. Roughly a third of candi-dates endorsed by Moms for Liberty won Tuesday or were leading in contested races, ac-cording to a Wall Street Jour-nal analysis. This was down from 45% in prior races. Tiffany Justice, co-founder of Moms for Liberty, said she was pleased with the results, even if most of the school board candidates the group backed lost, saying that most were running for the first time. Conservative candidates fared poorly in several closely watched races, including in Pennsylvania, a swing state where Democrats won majori-ties in some suburban districts. In Central Bucks, outside Philadelphia, the five-candidate Democratic slate swept the race and Republicans lost their 6-3 majority. Democrats had focused on two contentious board policies—one allowing parents to challenge school-li-brary books with sexual con-tent, and one barring teacher advocacy, including the dis-playing of objects such as rain-bow gay-pride flags. Re-elected Democrat Karen Smith called the outcome “a re-sounding rejection” of the poli-cies, which she said will be re-vised with input from all sides. “We need to set a positive example for kids. I will do ev-erything I can to change the tenor,” she said. Venture capitalist Paul Mar-tino, who pumped more than $200,000 into the Central Bucks race for the GOP slate that included his wife, said on Facebook that Republicans had a bad night across Pennsylva-nia. He also noted the Republi-can board slate was outspent. In Loudoun County, Va., where battles over Covid-19 masking policies and race-re-lated instruction drew national attention in recent years, can-didates backed by the local Democratic Party maintained a majority of the nine seats, all of which were on the ballot. The results suggest that the political potency of the conser-vative parents’ rights move-ment, which looked to be an emerging force before Tues-day’s elections, is an open question heading into the 2024 presidential election. Tuesday’s returns were a “mixed bag” overall, said Doug Kronaizl, who tracks school board races for Ballotpedia, a nonprofit elections site. The conservative backlash against how race and gender are taught seems to have drawn a backlash of its own, he said. Thousands of school boards across 21 states held elections this week, many of them un-contested or low-profile, ac-cording to Ballotpedia. In a number of the most conten-tious races, progressives were running against policies that conservatives enacted in the past two years, Kronaizl said.

Continued from Page One 

Firefighters called to extin-guish an electric-vehicle fire are discovering the surest ap-proach is to stand back and watch it burn. Electric cars combust dif-ferently than their gas-pow-ered counterparts. Firefight-ers and researchers said EV fires last longer, are harder to put out and have a tendency to reignite. First responders in Frank-lin, Tenn., faced their first burning EV in September, a Nissan Leaf that ignited while charging outside the car maker’s North America head-quarters. They spent hours pouring 45,000 gallons of wa-ter on the car, compared with the 500 to 1,000 gallons that fires involving gasoline-pow-ered vehicles usually need, Fire Marshal Andy King said. “I think if we were faced with a similar scenario next time, we might need to let it burn,” he said. Nissan said it is investigat-ing the cause of the fire. It isn’t clear how frequently EV fires take place, but as the cars become a larger part of the American fleet, some fire departments see them as a growing nuisance. Firefighters in Florida’s North Collier Fire Control and Rescue District responded to six last year af-ter a storm surge brought by Hurricane Ian caused saltwa-ter to get into EV battery compartments, which can cause short-circuits. There are more than 170,000 vehicle fires in the U.S. each year, but the Na-

# U.S. NEWS 

tional Fire Protection Associa-tion doesn’t break them out by power source. Tesla esti-mates its cars catch fire at a rate much lower than U.S. ve-hicles overall, and some inde-pendent studies have reached similar conclusions about EVs in general. First responders are still trying to figure out how to deal with EV fires. “When we look at how much money is going into battery plants, into the EV transition, there hasn’t been that carve-out to prepare the fire service,” said Michael O’Brian, fire chief of Michi-gan’s Brighton Area Fire Au-thority and chair of the Inter-national Association of Fire Chiefs’ battery committee. Electric-vehicle fires usu-ally start in the car’s lithium-ion batteries, which can store a massive amount of energy. If a battery is poorly designed, or if it is damaged by shock or internal spikes of lithium that form over time, a process known as “thermal runaway” can begin, said chief scientist Robert Slone of UL Solutions, which tests and certifies bat-teries. The batteries contain flam-mable chemicals and can re-lease their own oxygen as they burn, allowing EV fires to reignite hours or even days af-ter they appear to be quenched. They also emit toxic fumes that Tom Miller, who teaches for the West Vir-ginia University Fire Service Extension and the National Volunteer Fire Council, said “make hydrogen cyanide look like Pez.” Genevieve Cullen, president of the Electric Drive Transpor-tation Association industry group, said electric vehicle fires aren’t more dangerous than those involving gas-pow-ered vehicles, but do require their own tactics. “It’s a mat-ter of training,” she said. Motorists tempted to tackle an EV fire themselves should think twice, O’Brian said. A fire extinguisher would likely be incapable of tamping down the flames from a battery pack, he said, and the smoke is highly dangerous. He said drivers should pay attention to any indications a problem might be coming. “If the dash is telling you, ‘Don’t drive the car, pull over’—pull over and stop driving the car,” he said. Some departments take a cautious approach. Firefight-ers in Hillsborough Township, N.J., allowed a Tesla that had been in an accident to burn it-self out after it ignited in a salvage yard last month. The Cosumnes Fire Department in California did the same in May with a Tesla near Sacramento. That car’s owner, Bishal Malla, said it began vibrating harshly as he drove onto a highway ramp. Thinking he had a flat tire, he stopped and got out, only to see smoke bil-lowing from the undercar-riage. Within minutes, he said, the smoke became flames. A responding fire engine sprayed about 700 gallons of water on the fire, but when it was clear that the batteries were involved, Assistant Fire Chief Robert Kasparian told his firefighters to let it go. Tesla didn’t respond to a re-quest for comment. Kasparian said letting an EV burn isn’t always possible. He and his colleagues are try-ing to figure out how they would deal with an electric car that combusts in the owner’s garage. He said one idea is to hit the fire with a “fog stream,” which is a fine spray rather than a jet of wa-ter. That could lower the tem-perature enough so a fire-fighter could hook a chain to the car and drag it outside. New products aimed at taming EV fires are coming onto the market. The Louis-ville Fire Department in Ken-tucky has a specialized nozzle designed to slip under a car and spray water directly onto the undercarriage, cooling the battery pack. Others use large blankets meant to smother the flames. Audi has filed a patent ap-plication for a battery that can extinguish its own fire. In-dustrial conglomerate Honey-well and energy company Nex-ceris are creating early warning sensors for batteries, and 3M is working on materi-als meant to contain thermal runaway. 

# Electric-Car Fires Prove Hard to Put Out 

Vehicles combust differently; blazes last longer and have tendency to reignite 

Firefighters in Hillsborough Township, N.J., allowed an electric vehicle to burn itself out after it caught fire in a salvage yard last month.

founded the nonprofit United Poultry Concerns, other activ-ists urged her to consider a more sympathetic species, such as pigs. “My reaction to that was, well, all the more reason to start an organiza-tion for chickens, because they obviously need a voice,” Davis said in the 2006 documentary “Animal People.” That voice was heard in protests, petitions and lobby-ing efforts to end industry practices she saw as inhu-mane. One of her greatest suc-cesses came in 2004, when, after more than a decade of lobbying from Davis and other activists, the American Veteri-nary Medical Association took a public stand against “forced molting,” in which commercial egg producers deprive hens of food and water to induce an additional egg-laying cycle. The revised industry guide-lines improved the lives of millions of chickens. Davis wanted people to stop mistreating, killing and eating birds. But she also wanted them to stop think-ing—and speaking—ill of poul-try. That is what prompted her mid-1990s campaign against NPR’s “This American Life.” She believed its annual Thanksgiving “Poultry Slam” show—promoted as “stories of what happens when humans and fowls collide”—perpetu-ated stereotypes about birds that propagated the cultural acceptance of their slaughter, as she told host Ira Glass on the show in 1998. She persuaded Glass to visit her Virginia bird sanctu-ary. He came away converted. “In our earlier ‘Poultry Slams,’ three or four times, people referred to chickens as stupid, as having no personal-ity. In last year’s show, at one point, I myself declared that if chickens had a personality, that personality seemed to be that they’re a pain in the ass,” Glass said on the show. “Here’s one area where Karen Davis is completely in the right, and I and my radio staff have been absolutely wrong.” Glass ultimately gave up poultry altogether. “Every time after that, every time I ate a piece of chicken, I would think back to her chickens, and the shy ones and the out-going ones,” he said on “The Late Show With David Letter-man” in 2007. Karen Elizabeth Davis was born Feb. 4, 1944, in Altoona, Pa., where her mother was a French tutor and her father was an attorney—and ahunter. Growing up, Davis and her three younger brothers were avid meat eaters, she wrote. But she was also ahighly sensitive child who was inconsolable whenever a baby robin fell from its nest in her backyard. As a college student in the early 1960s, studying Russian and German history, she became so deeply dis-turbed by accounts of concen-tration camps that she began to fixate on the topic and dropped out of school. With the help of a psychia-trist, she overcame her obses-sion and graduated from col-lege in 1968. She went on to earn a doctoral degree in liter-ature from the University of Maryland, where she taught English for more than a de-cade. She married a fellow English professor, the late George Allan Cate. The mar-riage ended in divorce and the pair had no children. Davis soon found herself preoccupied with a new cause: animal rights. “Just as I be-came obsessed with concen-tration camps in the early 1960s, so in the early 1970s I began to agonize over the suf-fering and abuse of nonhuman animals,” she wrote. The fierceness of her advo-cacy occasionally ruffled feath-ers. “She was unyielding. But because she was brilliant and well-read, she never pushed the envelope beyond credibil-ity,” said Marc Bekoff, a profes-sor emeritus of ecology and evolutionary biology at the University of Colorado Boulder. Davis had been ill for two years, but she kept her prog-nosis private. “She didn’t want people worrying about her and interrupting her to see how she was doing,” said Franklin Wade, the vice presi-dent of United Poultry Con-cerns. “She had work to do.” Around this time of year, Karen Davis could be found in Washington, D.C., handing out leaflets proclaiming, “Turkeys Are Too Neat to Eat.” When she began campaign-ing for the rights of poultry more than four decades ago, she was essen-tially alone. Almost no one gave chickens or turkeys athought, ac-cording to Ingrid Newkirk, founder and president of Peo-ple for the Ethical Treatment of Animals. Davis, who died of cancer on Nov. 4 at age 79, became one of the loudest voices on their behalf—and was instru-mental in changing the way people perceive poultry. “Chickens were almost like cockroaches in public opinion; people didn’t think they counted,” Newkirk said. But Davis “never missed an oppor-tunity to show people that these were intelligent, loving, beautiful animals. She was one of the original pioneers who changed the conversation around chickens.” In 1990, when Davis

## Who We Are. 

Founded in the aftermath of 9/11, our mission began with honoring the legacy of my brother, Stephen Siller, a heroic NYC firefighter who sacrificed his life on 9/11 while helping to save others. Today, our foundation is one of the highest-rated charities in the country, with 95% of donations directly funding our programs. 

## What We Do. Our three primary missions: 

· Pay off mortgages for fallen first responders and military families, that leave behind young children. · Build "SMART homes" for catastrophically injured military and first responders. · Provide housing solutions and support for homeless veterans. 

## Our Next Promise. 

We estimate 50,000 veterans are homeless. This is unacceptable. When they were in the military, no one would have dared to leave them behind. So why, why is it okay now that they're on American soil for them to live like this? That's why we initiated our Homeless Veteran Program. We have started building Veteran Villages in multiple cities throughout the nation that provide a home with comprehensive care and support our veterans' need. Since this program's inception we've housed over 2,000 veterans and aim to support 3,500 by the end of 2023.

China’s Ministry of Com-merce, the Ministry of Indus-try and Information Technol-ogy and the National Development and Reform Commission—the country’s top economic planner—didn’t respond to questions from The Wall Street Journal. Prices of goods shipped from China have fallen around 20% this year, according to ABN AMRO. While some of that drop reflects easing sup-ply-chain bottlenecks, it is also a sign that Chinese sell-ers are discounting to pre-serve or expand market share during a period of weaker global demand, according to economists. Local governments in China have been subsidizing trips abroad for companies to sell more overseas, including char-tering flights for them. They are urging banks to lend to companies that want to ex-pand in countries participat-ing in China’s Belt and Road program. Beijing has also called on financial institutions to direct credit to the manufacturing sector. In an August report, Gold-man Sachs singled out several products that are oversupplied in China, including batteries, excavators and some chemicals. Weltmeister, a Chinese electric-car brand owned by Shanghai-based WM Motor Technology, was operating at a loss for years and at one point stopped production at one of its factories. That plant restarted in June after the lo-cal government provided fi-nancial support, the company said. The factory was aimed at “expediting the delivery of overseas orders so as to ex-pand their global market share,” said Kang Yun, a com-pany representative. “With a weakening econ-omy, China naturally looks for exports,” said Brad Setser, a scholar at the Council on For-eign Relations. “But any meaningful expansion of Chi-nese exports beyond current levels will crush production elsewhere.” Excess Chinese production also has upsides, including the potential to help lower infla-tion. JPMorgan estimates that falling producer prices in China will lower global core goods in-flation outside the country by 0.7 percentage point over the second half of 2023. Many of China’s excess goods are being snapped up eagerly. Cheaper EVs, batter-ies and solar panels from China can help the U.S. and other countries achieve their greenhouse emission reduc-tion goals. China’s share of global EV exports grew from 4% in 2020 to 21% in 2022, it said. While many of those ex-ports are foreign brands that produce in China, especially Tesla, Chinese brands, with their lower sticker prices, are becoming a bigger part of the picture. Their share of total EU EV sales grew from 0.5% in 2019 to over 8% so far in 2023, according to Schmidt Automo-The U.S. and China made it official, announcing that Presi-dent Biden and Chinese leader Xi Jinping will meet Wednes-day for a summit that both powers say they want to keep their rivalry in check. The summit in the San Fran-cisco Bay Area will be the lead-ers’ first face-to-face meeting in a year. It comes after a pe-riod of spiraling tensions over technology, U.S. support for Taiwan and other issues, and follows months of maneuvering by both governments to stabi-lize ties. In confirming the much-an-ticipated meeting, senior Bi-den administration officials on Friday previewed a lengthy 

BY CHARLES HUTZLER 

AND BRIAN SPEGELE 

# WORLD NEWS 

agenda of issues they expect will come up, including the Is-rael-Hamas war, North Korea and China’s backing of Russia during its war on Ukraine. For substantive outcomes, the U.S. and China look close to reaching agreements on key Washington asks, though Bi-den administration officials declined to confirm any prog-ress. Both sides have moved closer to resuming contacts between their militaries. The Pentagon has accused China of making dangerous intercepts of U.S. and allied craft to push them out of the region. The U.S. is also seeking Bei-jing’s help in choking off ex-ports of chemicals shipped to Mexico where they are used by drug cartels to make fentanyl. For Beijing, Xi wants breathing room in relations with the U.S. as he tries to deal with a weakened econ-omy, stem an outflow of for-eign capital and cope with tightened American restric-tions on technology transfers. China also seeks assurances on Taiwan, the self-ruled dem-ocratic island that Beijing claims. Chinese officials want Biden to commit to opposing Taiwan’s formal independence, according to people briefed on the preparations. Beijing’s firmly held position that the U.S. needs to do more to accommodate China as a rising power, reiterated repeatedly by Chinese officials and in Chinese media, is one reason experts don’t expect a breakthrough. Part of Beijing’s goal will be to drive home the message for the U.S. that it doesn’t seek to undermine global peace and stability. That message never-theless faces skepticism by U.S. officials, especially as China clashes with its neighbors in the South China Sea and has aligned itself more with Russia. Both governments have set modest expectations for the summit. U.S.-China relations plum-meted this past year, first over disagreements about Tai-wan and then over a Chinese balloon that the Biden admin-istration said was conducting surveillance over North Amer-ica before it was shot down. High-level contacts, which dwindled during the pan-demic, then all but stopped. Chinese-language commen-taries by the official Xinhua News Agency this week praised the recent stabiliza-tion of ties between the coun-tries, but blamed the U.S. for their deterioration this year. The root of the problem is that “the United States has deviated in its perceptions of China, of the world, and of it-self,” one of the Xinhua com-mentaries said. Getting Beijing to agree to a summit involved complicated diplomatic choreography. Part of that involved Treasury Sec-retary Janet Yellen, who Friday ended two days of meetings with Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng, a trusted Xi lieutenant, aimed at airing economic and national security issues. Yellen was among a parade of senior officials to Beijing to open up discussions. In pivotal meetings two weeks ago in Washington, China’s most se-nior foreign policy official, Wang Yi, struck an “agree-ment in principle” for a sum-mit, officials said, but held just shy of a full commitment. A reason for the reluctance, Chinese officials said, is con-cern that the Biden adminis-tration would do something counter to Beijing’s interests— such as making an arms sale to Taiwan or putting sanctions on a major Chinese company— and that would embarrass Xi. Chinese diplomats have urged the U.S. to keep protesters away from Xi’s motorcade, hotel and the summit venue, accord-ing to officials on both sides.

tive Research. Western producers are sounding alarms over cheap renewable-energy products, including wind turbines and solar panels. Chinese exports of solar panels increased by around one-third year over year in the first half of 2023, to 114 giga-watts, equivalent to the total installed solar panel capacity of the U.S., according to Em-ber, an independent energy think tank. In Europe, solar module prices fell by 50% within four weeks starting late last year even though market funda-mentals were largely un-changed during that period, said Gunter Erfurt, chief exec-utive officer of Meyer Burger Technology, a Switzerland-based manufacturer of solar cells and modules. To Erfurt, it was a sign China was discounting at what he called “unprecedented speed” to grab market share. Another industry econo-mists are watching closely is steel. China, the world’s biggest producer, is likely to see de-mand fall 1.1% this year from a year earlier, in part because of sluggish housing construction, according to forecasts by the China Iron and Steel Associa-tion in July. Production contin-ues to rise, it said. China’s steel export prices have plunged about 60% from a year earlier, while its steel exports volume went up 53% in October compared with 2022, according to Frederic Neumann, chief Asia econo-mist at HSBC. Some Chinese factories, saddled with overcapacity in a struggling economy, are trying to export their way out of trouble and stoking new trade tensions in the process. Makers of electric vehicles, solar panels and other prod-ucts are cutting prices and try-ing harder to muscle into over-seas markets as they face weakened demand at home, upsetting competitors who see threats to their bottom lines. The tensions are most acute in Europe, where Euro-pean Union regulators in Sep-tember unveiled an antisub-sidy probe, reflecting concern that China is flooding the re-gion with low-cost electric ve-hicles. The U.S. recently announced levies on tin-plate metal prod-ucts from China and two other countries, after determining that their steelmakers were selling at unfairly low prices. India is investigating whether China dumped arange of goods, from chemi-cals to furniture parts, into the country at unfair prices. Vietnam in September started examining whether wind tow-ers imported from China have hurt domestic manufacturers. Chinese officials have said that the country’s manufac-turers are competing fairly and that their products are gaining market share overseas because they are attractive to foreign buyers. > CHINA DAILY/REUTERS

NEW DELHI—The fierce ri-valry between India and China for influence in the Indian Ocean is on display in the tiny archipelago nation of Mal-dives, better known for its white sand beaches and crys-tal clear waters than as a stra-tegic military outpost. The Maldives is awash with Chinese loans, which have helped fund new high rises on an artificial island, roads and a landmark bridge. Now Indian loans and grants are behind a $500 million bridge project connecting three islands to the capital city of Malé. “It is our answer to the Chinese,” said an Indian official with direct knowledge of the country’s de-velopment spending. The money pouring into the Maldives, a string of 26 atolls about 350 miles from India’s west coast, reflects a wider fight for clout occurring be-tween India and China across South and Southeast Asia. China, with its deep pockets, has become the world’s big-gest development financier, much of it through its Belt and Road Initiative. But India is putting billions of dollars toward infrastructure projects and assistance in countries such as Sri Lanka, Bangladesh and Myanmar. “New Delhi has been at-tempting to counter Beijing’s strategy by maintaining close ties to bordering nations, with 

# India, China Fight for Influence Over Maldives 

the exception of Pakistan, and proposing its own access and development agreements to di-minish Chinese influence,” said Derek Grossman, a defense an-alyst focusing on the Indo-Pa-cific at Rand, a U.S. think tank. “However, local elections have occasionally swung the pendu-lum in China’s favor.” The Maldives sits in the middle of major shipping lanes connecting East and West Asia. It is also part of a contest for influence in the region that is seen as central to Washing-ton’s strategy to remain domi-nant in the Indo-Pacific arena. India, which has drawn close to the U.S., is the top military power in the Indian Ocean. But India’s efforts recently were dealt a setback as the Maldives’s new president-elect has vowed to oust Indian secu-rity forces from the islands. Mohamed Muizzu, who is set to take office Nov. 17, is seen as part of the island’s pro-Beijing political camp, which had been out of power for five years. Since U.S. ties with the ar-chipelago are still limited—it only established an embassy in Malé this year—Washington is likely to rely on India to en-sure that China doesn’t gain a military foothold in the Mal-dives, said Grossman. In 2019, New Delhi got per-mission to base a radar-sur-veillance system in the Mal-dives. The system tracks vessel movements and feeds into an Indian Navy maritime-security information-sharing hub near New Delhi that includes repre-sentatives from a dozen na-tions, said a security official and ex-Indian Navy official. Muizzu and his party tapped into sentiment among some voters who see the India presence as an affront to Mal-dives sovereignty, assisting his victory over President Ibrahim Mohamed Solih, the pro-India incumbent. “The people have decided they don’t want the presence of foreign soldiers,” said Muizzu at a victory rally in October, without naming In-dia. The two countries don’t disclose the specific number of Indian troops stationed there. A spokesman for India’s Foreign Ministry said last month that India looks for-ward to engaging with the new administration. A serving senior Maldivian diplomat, who didn’t want to be identified, said the new leadership should avoid antag-onizing a longtime ally such as India. “India’s security con-cerns in the Indian Ocean can’t be overlooked,” he said. The tug of war the Mal-dives is witnessing is playing out across the South Asian countries that ring India. Un-der Prime Minister Narendra Modi, India has worked to strengthen ties with key neigh-bors, said Ashok Malik, chair of the India practice at the Asia Group, a strategic advisory firm. “The competition be-tween India and China in the Indian Ocean region has un-doubtedly become more ener-getic,” he said. “The Modi gov-ernment has sought to match China’s projects and influence by playing to India’s strengths.”

tions filed in Europe. The influx is costing the country some €50 billion a year, equivalent to $53 billion, roughly the size of its defense budget, and has sparked asharp rise in the ratings of the far-right Alternative for Ger-many, the country’s second most popular political party. Cutting this cost isn’t straightforward because Ger-man courts have ruled that refugees are entitled to the same welfare benefits as Ger-man citizens. Earlier this week, Chancel-lor Olaf Scholz unveiled what he called a “historic” deal be-tween the three parties in his coalition and the conservative opposition on stricter migra-tion measures, including ex-ploring the idea of using third countries to relocate some mi-grants, speeding up asylum procedures to turn back those who fail to qualify, and re-stricting benefits for refugees. Scholz, his closest aides and some of his key ministers have been exploring deals to reroute some refugee flows through Africa for months and are drafting offers to various governments, officials with knowledge of the plans told The Wall Street Journal. Officials have held initial talks with countries such as Kenya in the past but couldn’t make them a concrete offer because they lacked a man-date from the coalition, which includes the pro-asylum Green Party. Representatives of Kenya, Ghana, Senegal and Morocco didn’t respond to a request for comment. “Our common goal is to push back this irregular immi-gration,” Scholz told reporters this week. Germany, he said, must start to deport rejected asylum seekers—about 50% of all applicants—at a “grand scale.” Italy’s government also said this week it struck a deal with Albania to have asylum seek-ers picked up at sea stay in that Balkan country while their applications are consid-ered in Italy. Applicants who are accepted would be moved to Italy while the others could be held up to 18 months be-fore being repatriated to their home country. The U.K. government, meanwhile, is fighting a legal battle to be allowed to send some asylum seekers to Rwanda. British judges ac-cepted the principle that sending refugees to third countries is legal but said Rwanda didn’t have a suffi-ciently developed asylum sys-tem to deal with the requests. The U.K.’s high court is ex-pected to issue a final ruling next week. The German plan to relo-cate some asylum seekers to Africa could involve the United Nations High Commis-sioner for Refugees overseeing the process with funding from Berlin and other Western na-tions, officials said. U.N. in-volvement would ensure that any such agreement doesn’t breach Germany’s legal obliga-tions toward refugees and withstands possible court challenges. Berlin also is working on renewing a landmark deal with Turkey that has limited illegal migration transiting through the country. Berlin wants to be able to send back large numbers of asylum seek-ers who reach Germany via Turkey in exchange for pay-ments, and political and trade concessions. Scholz said efforts to re-duce migration face obstacles, including legal hurdles and objections raised by third countries that could act as a temporary destination for some migrants. “At the end of the day it will be largely dependent on finding someone that is will-ing to follow this through to-gether,” Scholz said.

MEXICO 

Apple Co-Founder Suffered Stroke 

Apple co-founder Steve Wozniak said he suffered a minor stroke while attending a business conference in Mexico City. Wozniak told ABC News in a text Thursday that he felt dizzy Wednesday morning, then experienced vertigo be-fore going to the hospital where an MRI revealed he had had a “minor but real stroke.” Wozniak, 73 years old, had been scheduled to speak at the World Business Forum in Mex-ico City, a two-day gathering. Wozniak, who teamed up with the late Steve Jobs to found Apple in 1976, had been scheduled as the con-ference’s closing speaker Wednesday afternoon. Wozniak told the New York Times that he was re-leased from the hospital Thursday, flew back to Cali-fornia and was waiting for dinner at home in Los Gatos. “I’m back home and feeling good,” Wozniak said. Wozniak left Apple in 1985 to pursue a range of other in-terests, but has remained a fervent supporter of the com-pany and a technology evan-gelist. —Associated Press 

FRANCE 

Le Pen Is Criticized For Plan to March 

French far-right leader Ma-rine Le Pen has set off a ca-cophony of criticism over her plans to attend a weekend march to protest rising anti-semitism in France, with crit-ics saying that her once-pariah party has failed to shake off its antisemitic heritage despite growing political legitimacy. Prime Minister Élisabeth Borne, numerous political parties and citizens are to at-tend the Sunday march. Le Pen has said that she and her National Rally party also will be there, in what some see as an attempt to lever-age the Israel-Hamas war to make herself more palatable to mainstream voters. Party founder Jean-Marie Le Pen, her father, was con-victed repeatedly of antise-mitic hate speech and played down the scope of the Holo-caust. Daughter Marine—runner-up in the last two presiden-tial elections and likely a top contender in 2027—has worked to scrub the party’s image, kicking her father out and changing its name from National Front to National Rally. 

—Associated Press 

NORWAY 

Charges Filed in Oslo Pride Attack 

A Norwegian citizen origi-nally from Iran was Friday charged with aggravated ter-rorism for the 2022 deadly shooting ahead of an LGBTQ festival in the nightlife dis-trict of the capital, Oslo. Two people were killed and nine seriously wounded in the shooting at three loca-tions, chiefly outside the Lon-don Pub, a popular gay bar, on June 25, 2022. Prosecutor Sturla Henriksbø said that Zaniar Matapour, 44, fired 10 rounds with a machine gun and eight shots with a handgun into a crowd at a street corner in the nightlife district where there were a to-tal of 560 people. Before that, he took “an oath of allegiance to the Islamic State group.” “The attack caused great fear,” Henriksbø said. Matapour was arrested shortly after by bystanders, and has since been held on suspicion of being the shooter. Another prosecutor, Aud Kinsarvik Gravås, said four other suspects were linked to the shooting but they haven’t been charged as the investi-gation is continuing. 

—Associated Press 

BERLIN—Germany is work-ing on a plan to send some asylum seekers to Africa while their cases are decided, mark-ing a U-turn for a country that has long been open to mi-grants but is straining under a surge in arrivals. Berlin is considering ap-proaching Kenya, Ghana, Sen-egal, Morocco and others to house some asylum seekers while they await approval of their applications, a process that can sometimes take years, according to several German officials familiar with the discussions. The plans, which are still being negoti-ated, could include perma-nently resettling those who fail to win refugee status in those countries, officials said, as well as motivating those with some right to protection in Germany to settle in third countries. The shift in Germany is part of a broader move among more industrialized nations such as the U.S., the U.K. and Italy to tighten their migra-tion rules to reduce an in-crease in the numbers of mi-grants, especially those seeking asylum. In the U.S., for instance, more than two million people crossed the border illegally in each of the past two years—many of them seeking asylum. The U.K. gov-ernment is hoping to send asylum seekers to Rwanda. The surge has sparked po-litical tensions in many coun-tries and swamped asylum systems, leading to yearslong 

Humanitarian Crisis Is Looming 

Germany has begun to crack down on immigration in recent weeks, accelerat-ing deportations, restricting family reunification for ac-cepted asylum seekers, and reinstating border checks with its European Union neighbors. It will also re-duce benefits for asylum seekers and make some benefits available through a payment card to discour-age migrants from wiring money outside Germany, and increase the share of benefits paid out in kind. Berlin only deports a few thousand rejected ap-plicants every year. Many deportations fail either be-cause migrants don’t have travel documents, disap-pear or call in sick shortly before their deportation, or because their countries re-fuse to take them back. At a meeting between Germany’s federal and state governments this week, local officials com-plained that many commu-nities can’t absorb more migrants. Policy makers have about a year to shift the migration paradigm by im-plementing their new plans if they want to avoid politi-cal turmoil and a humani-tarian crisis, said Gerald Knaus, an immigration re-searcher who advises the government.

The last neighborhoods of Mosul were retaken by Iraqi forces after a campaign that took almost nine months, killed thousands of people and displaced nearly a million ci-vilians. The U.S. intelligence com-munity doubts that Israel can achieve its stated military goal of eliminating the U.S.-desig-nated terrorist group, a per-son familiar with the intelli-gence said. While the campaign can damage Hamas and its infrastructure, it can-not eliminate the ideology, the person said. “I think a more realistic goal is to buy security for a matter of years, but not forever,” a congressional official said. Israeli troops and armored vehicles have sliced through the center of the Gaza Strip, cut-ting Gaza City off from the rest of the enclave. Tens of thousands of Gaza residents have fled, many say-ing the fighting and shortages of food and water have made it impossible to stay. The In-ternational Committee of the Red Cross said Friday that the health system in the Gaza Strip has collapsed. “Every day’s bombing is worse than the day before,” said Hazem Abu Ghalyoun, a Gaza resident, who said he and his family took refuge in a school but had to depart as the fighting drew closer. “We are forced to flee.
