lications, the latter a scholar at the American Enterprise Institute—are men of the center-left, and their contentions accept liberal premises. This is particu-larly true of the first part of their argument (there are two). To oversim-plify: Beginning in the 1970s with Jimmy Carter, and then in the ’90s with Bill Clinton and the Democratic Leader-ship Council, Democrats abandoned unions, embraced free trade and loose immigration policies, and staffed their administrations with Wall Street executives who championed relatively tight money policies and financial deregulation. American manufacturing collapsed, Democrats became the party of the wealthy and educated, and an im-poverished working class was left aban-doned by its party and vulnerable to the appeals of cultural reaction. There’s some truth in that interpre-tation, but some of it reads like a con-clusion in search of evidence. The idea that trade barriers could have preserved American manufacturing from Europe’s resurgence and the rise of East Asia is fanciful. If Democrats had wanted to keep manufacturing at home, they should have kept taxes low and regula-tion minimal. They did the opposite. Bye-bye, Maytag. In the book’s second half, Messrs. Judis and Teixeira explain how a“shadow party” has moved the Demo-crats to the left. Shadow party is their term for the activist organizations, think tanks, foundations, publications, donor networks, commentators and academics who shape the formal party’s positions and outlook. “The labor movement used to play a dominant role in the Demo-crats’ shadow party and kept it rooted in working-class concerns,” they write. 

The Democrats have become the party of the college-educated; working-class voters look to the Republicans. 

During the Clinton and Obama years, unions took a back seat to “Hollywood, Silicon Valley, and Wall Street, together with various environmental, civil rights, and feminist groups.” In the 2020s, the authors observe, the party is dominated by a host of left-progressive organiza-tions and companies that cater to hyper-educated urban progressives: the ACLU, Black Lives Matter, MSNBC, Vox, the New York Times, the Ford Foundation and the Center for American Progress, among many others. Messrs. Judis and Teixeira think unions were done in by an alliance between “business Republicans” and Clintonian Democrats. In my view they did themselves in by making demands that made sense in 1950 but amounted to economic suicide a half-century later. The authors’ contention, however, that the left’s media, nonprofits and founda-tions have made the Democratic Party odious to the average non-college-educated voter seems unassailably true. Today’s media-driven conventional wisdom holds that the GOP has moved dramatically to the right. This is to con-fuse the admittedly absurd tactics of congressional Republicans over the past decade with political extremism. Sub-stantive extremism exists almost totally on the Democratic side. The last four chapters of Messrs. Judis and Teixeira’s book chronicle the Democratic Party’s embrace of “antiracism,” open-borders anarchy, trans ideology and climate pol-icies that would—and still may—destroy the American economy. Where have the Democrats gone? Mad, that’s where. 

“Party of the People” (Simon & Schuster, 336 pages, $30) , by the Republican pollster Patrick Ruffini, addresses the same phenomenon— working-class voters’ defection—but from the other party’s viewpoint. Mr. Ruffini, too, begins his book with a confession. After Mitt Romney lost in 2012, an assemblage of Republican strat-egists produced a report—the press called it an “autopsy”—in which they concluded that the GOP lost because it took a hard line on immigration and failed to appeal to Hispanic voters. Mr. Trump’s 2016 campaign seemed per-versely to reject the report’s counsel. Beltway Republicans like Mr. Ruffini were sure he would lose. Reflecting on his mistake soon after-ward, he writes: “I began to realize that my background and life experiences themselves were the problem. They had blinded me to an accurate understand-ing of the American electorate. . . . I only saw the swing voters that might be repulsed by Trump, not those drawn to him. And it was all because of a fatal, reality-blinding flaw I shared with almost everyone in politics and the media: I graduated from college.” Most American adults do not, in fact, hold college degrees. “Party of the People” is full of granular discussions of demographic trends, bar graphs and dot charts, slopes and intercepts. Some of the analysis—as long as we’re airing confessions here—I had to take on faith.

# Everyone Switch Places 

# IN 2002, John B. Judis and Ruy Teixeira published a book whose thesis seemed calculated to tempt liberals into thinking they’d discovered electoral fairy dust. “The Emerging Democratic Major-ity” argued that four demographic groups were trending strongly toward the center-left: professionals, women, minorities and blue-collar workers. The election of Barack Obama in 2008 seemed to confirm their thesis, and soon young Democratic strategists and pundits had reduced Messrs. Judis and Teixeira’s nuanced and complex argu-ment to the formulation “demography is destiny.” In other words: We win, no matter what! Subsequent elections, especially the midterm years of 2010 and 2014, in which the GOP made large gains in Congress, suggested that things weren’t going to be so simple. Donald Trump’s victory in 2016, in which the white working class abandoned the Democrats in droves, exploded the thesis entirely. In “Where Have All the Democrats Gone?” (Holt, 336 pages, $29.99) ,Messrs. Judis and Teixeira freely confess that they failed to foresee the working-class defection, but they’re right to note that the rest of their argu-ment held. Professionals, women and minorities have grown and largely hewed Democratic. The newer book purports to describe why and how the Democratic Party lost its core constitu-ency—the average working man—and fell under the sway of knowledge-class progressives and radical activists. Messrs. Judis and Teixeira—the former a journalist long associated with the New Republic and other liberal pub-

BY BARTON SWAIM 

P2JW315000-4-C00900-1--------XA C10 | Saturday/Sunday, November 11 - 12, 2023 * * * * THE WALL STREET JOURNAL. 

BY BRAD LEITHAUSER 

# YASUNARI KAWABATA 

was not the first mod-ern Japanese novelist to be translated into Eng-lish, but for many Amer-ican readers he introduced a nation’s literary sensibility. When he was awarded the Nobel prize in 1968, he became the first Japanese laureate, an ascendancy that coincided with a crest-ing wave of exports from his country to the West—steel and cars and transistor radios, movies and toys and art. It’s no surprise that Kawabata was viewed as a benchmark: Here it was, the modern Japanese novel. The first to arrive was “Snow Coun-try,” in 1956, followed by “Thousand Cranes,” in 1958. Both novels were translated by Edward G. Seidensticker, whose introduction to “Snow Country” sought to assist Western readers who might find its trappings—country inns and geishas, kimonos and sake cups— disorienting. Seidensticker pointed out Kawabata’s ties to Japanese poetry: This was prose of a sere, haiku-like del-icacy and suggestiveness, with much implied and little specified. Japan’s wartime defeat and rapid transformation mirrored experiences Kawabata had earlier in his own life. Born in Osaka in 1899, orphaned by the age of 4, he was raised by his paternal grandparents, both of whom had died by the time he was 15. He briefly moved in with his mother’s family, but soon, reinventing himself as a student in Tokyo, was on his own. In short or-der, he found himself a comfortingly stable family in the house of literature. Kawabata took up fiction writing. What’s most striking about his “chil-dren”—his characters—is the detach-ment he shows them. You might say he respects their privacy, leaving most of their intimacies undisclosed. They are stylized figures moving through styl-ized landscapes. They are, typically, loners. Often, their most profound link-ages are not to other people but to the glories of the natural world—bamboo groves, camellia blossoms, hills and valleys and rainbows. “The Rainbow” is in fact the English title of Kawabata’s 1950 novel, in a new translation by Haydn Trowell, set dur-ing the last years of the postwar occu-pation of Japan. In some ways it’s a typical Kawabata offspring: A gentle watercolor wash of melancholia over-shadows the book’s to-ings and fro-ings. In the war’s aftermath, Kawabata famously declared that henceforth he could write nothing but elegies. But even his prewar books focused primar-ily on what was departing rather than arriving. Life is fragile, he constantly reminds us, and our hold upon it pre-carious. In “The Rainbow,” as in so many of his novels, there is much dis-cussion of suicide. (It appears likely, but not certain, that Kawabata took his own life when he died in 1972 at the age of 72. He’d already silenced himself by ceasing publication after receiving his Nobel.) In significant ways, however, this fine novel is full of surprises. While the war is the spiny watershed in the land-scape of Kawabata’s fiction, shaping the downward path many of his plots follow, his novels rarely address it di-rectly. Yet in “The Rainbow” political issues run close to the surface—its characters speak openly of the atomic bomb and the dangers of conflicts in Korea. It chronicles a topsy-turvy era when, as Kawabata’s narrator remarks, “the houses of former nobles were be-ing transformed into inns and restau-rants.” The protagonist of “The Rainbow,” Mizuhara Tsuneo, is a typical Kawabata hero, possessing both an artistic career (he is an eminent architect) and a tan-gled romantic life (his three daughters are by three different women). Typical, too, are his backward-looking profes-sional qualms (“his doubts that mod-ern architecture, compared to that of the past, would endure as a testament to beauty”) and the webs of familial se-crecy that keep his daughters ignorant of one another. Yet the sympathy and solidity with which the young women are etched is unusual in Kawabata’s oeuvre, and the book’s nifty, suspense-ful plotting is rare, too, in a writer of-ten more concerned with ambience than events. The reader watches in fas-cination as the lives of the three half-sisters, for all their separations of ge-ography and blood, increasingly come to interweave. Numerous translations of Kawabata have appeared since “Snow Country” and “Thousand Cranes” arrived in the 1950s, slowly clarifying just what an iso-lated and peculiar benchmark he pro-vided. He was never offering us the 

modern Japanese novel. His was a highly personal, indeed quirky, take on his country as it propelled itself through and beyond the ravages of war. “The Rainbow” adds a valuable layer to the portrait of the artist re-vealed by his work. It shows us a tem-perament in transition, as Kawabata 

# BOOKS 

‘Truth is in “the discarding of words.” It lies “outside words.” ’ —YASUNARI KAWABATA 

The Rainbow 

By Yasunari Kawabata 

Vintage, 224 pages, $17 

confronted the war with a directness he would later abandon. A heart-pierc-ing confession arrives when Mizuhara’s daughter Momoko, who is a bit of a lost soul, introduces a man met on the street to her sister: “This is Aoki’s brother. Aoki was my . . . lover. He died in Okinawa, in the war.” In contrast to “The Rainbow,” Kawa-bata’s later fiction represented a steady pulling away from the here-and-now. In 1965’s “Beauty and Sadness,” the last novel he saw to publication, the tolling of the past, as embodied by the colos-sal bell at Kyoto’s Chion-in temple, has far more resonance and reality than the wristwatches and alarm clocks that oversee the life of its Tokyo-based hero. Having read most everything of his that has been translated, I sometimes find puzzling my own devotion to Kawabata. It’s certainly not rooted in his depths of portraiture, his handling of action, his bonhomie, his sense of humor. Yet if his tone is elegiac, one never doubts the deep spiritual ache of this softspoken observer, or the nonpa-reil splendors of the receding world he would memorialize. Perhaps the prime pleasure in his books is rooted in language—a plea-sure that can be felt despite the obsta-cles of translation between two such different narrative traditions. His is perishable linguistic cargo, and doubt-less much is lost whenever one of his novels sails across the Pacific. And yet an unignorable spiritual urgency reli-ably survives the journey. His works speak of a domain all the brighter for being descried at one remove, the way pine trees may be, as one of this novel’s character’s finds, “more vivid in hue reflected on the river than they were in the world above.” In his Nobel acceptance speech, Kawabata declared: “The single flower contains more brightness than a hun-dred flowers.” He was a minimalist, whose work embraces minimalism’s hopeful assumption that, in the right hands, a string of minute details—a phrase, an unspoken gesture, a linking of gazes—may unlock a multitude of meanings. Look closely, listen carefully ,is the first tacit message of Kawabata’s novels. The second is, Let my story burrow inward . There is more here than meets the eye and ear. 

Mr. Leithauser is the author, most recently, of “Rhyme’s Rooms: The Architecture of Poetry.” 

# Quiet Revolution 

RECOGNITION Yasunari Kawabata in 1968, after receiving the Nobel Prize for Literature. 

> KEYSTONE-FRANCE/GETTY IMAGES

The minimalist writer Yasunari Kawabata rendered the changes of postwar Japan with an elegant spareness. 

Wives of U.S. staff in Vietnam during the 1960s venture into the restive country. 

IN 1963, Alice McDermott reminds us in her novel “Abso-lution” (FSG, 324 pages, $28) ,both the president of the United States, John F. Kennedy, and the president of Vietnam, Ngo Dinh Diem, were Catholics. This fact holds a holy significance for the engineer and military consultant Peter Kelly and his wife, Patricia, Catholic newlyweds who move to Saigon that year. To these true believers, the two countries’ anticommunist alliance is “divinely ordained,” and the downfall of both leaders—Diem was assassinated in a CIA-funded coup and Kennedy was killed only weeks later—augurs for evil times ahead. But “Absolution” is not about the politics of American intervention in Vietnam. It instead concentrates on the parallel story of those American wives whose diplomatic mission is to oversee a household staff, host garden parties, provide for their husbands’ relaxation and uphold Western standards of spousal comportment. The novel is narrated mostly by Patricia, who in old age relives her mem-ories in a letter to the daughter of her friend Charlene. If, in 1963, Patricia is the earnest naïf of the claque of housewives, Charlene is the wild card. Sardonic, streetwise, bossy and tenacious, she lures Patricia into a series of charitable schemes that take them far outside the gated confines of their Saigon neighborhood. Charlene and Patricia’s proj-ects begin modestly, as they push their friends to buy their children bespoke Vietnamese Barbie accessories (hand-stitched by a Vietnamese house-keeper, of course), using the pro-ceeds for gifts for hospitalized children. In time, as they begin visits to a leper colony and in-volve themselves in the under-ground adoption market, their altruism becomes more forward and more morally questionable. Charlene’s aggressive do-good-ing is impressive but high-handed in the colonialist man-ner. Patricia’s motives are ambiguous, too. She was raised in the Catholic tradition of ser-vice to the less fortunate, yet an unstable personal yearning for the baby she has been unable to bear inflames her interest in Vietnamese children. Ms. McDermott explores such vagaries with grace and consideration. Patricia’s thoughts always return, with great sensitivity, to the problem of goodness, which can appear inextricable from self-interest or mere docility. Of her many temptations in Vietnam, the simplest would be to fall back to her assigned role as an obedient housewife, who is not expected to grapple with dilemmas of conscience. These engaging reflections are mixed with, and to some extent diluted by, a ubiquitous note of apology. Patricia knows —as does the reader—that history has judged her very presence in Saigon to be ethically wrong, so she is anxious about even attempting to evoke sympathy for her life there. “You have to remember the times,” she is prone to pleading, and the fear of sounding hopelessly out of touch lends something tentative to the narrative—many provocative things almost happen but in the end are left largely hypothetical. This practice of begging the reader’s indulgence rather than simply telling a story is common in fiction today, but it is even more noticeable in writing as delicate and lovely as Ms. McDermott’s. Michael Cunningham’s novel 

“Day” (Random House, 288 pages, $28) returns to the author’s preferred milieu of artistic white Manhattanites. Here again is a discontented middle-aged couple, named Isabel and Dan. (In 2010’s “By Nightfall” they were called Peter and Rebecca; in 2014’s “The Snow Queen,” they were Tyler and Beth.) They share their home with Isabel’s gay brother, Robbie, who has acted as a kind of Band-Aid to the failing marriage but is now planning to move out. (The talismanic gay brother in “By Nightfall” is named Ethan; in “The Snow Queen,” it’s Barrett.) Set over three separate days in 2019 and 2021, and involving the tragic effects of the Covid pandemic, the novel flashes upon the pair’s regrets and dreams for transformation. It is, as noted, a formula that Mr. Cunningham has applied to great success. But the apology bug appears to have bitten him, too. The characters’ meditations are now accompanied by the requisite handwringing about “white lady problems” and “decadent unhappiness.” Mr. Cunningham even works in a dig at his own touchstone influence, Virginia Woolf, by including a classroom scene in which a student complains (inaccurately) that “modernists were all rich white people.” The ironic distancing is lethal to a writer who relies so much on sensory immersion. I usually begin Mr. Cunningham’s books rolling my eyes at his cossetted characters but finish them thoroughly won over, enchanted by the magic of the prose. With “Day,” which feels flatter and more ballasted by present sensibilities, my skepticism never dispersed. The stories in Yiyun Li’s collection “Wednesday’s Child” (FSG, 241 pages, $27) are post-traumatic. The characters, most of them Chinese-Americans, are returning to society in the aftermath of a tragedy that would seem to make such a return impossible —in some cases, the suicide of a child. But what is markedly unusual about Ms. Li’s writing is that it rarely sinks into despair. Despite their background matter, the stories burble along with a facade of affability, cheerfully documenting the interactions of everyday routines. The incongruous mood may reflect a survival instinct, what one mother thinks of as the defense mechanism of “skimming on life’s surface.” Or it may be a kind of learned stoicism. Staying positive is like having imaginary friends, argues a woman in “Such Common Life.” “If I decide I have optimism, there I have it.” It is, then, a fragile optimism, and Ms. Li’s art is in revealing glimpses of the shadows underneath it without hauling them to the surface, like darkness glimpsed beneath a crust of ice. Naivete and avoidance come to seem like dynamic, if insufficient, qualities. “Oh, when children are out in the world, parents are fated to doubt; yet when no doubt is left for them, nothing good is left, either,” she writes. In this strange and distinctive collection, garrulity is a cover for a deeper speechlessness and hope is a disguise for fatalism.

By Michael Cunningham

FICTION 

SAM SACKS 

P2JW315000-4-C01000-1--------XA THE WALL STREET JOURNAL. * * * * Saturday/Sunday, November 11 - 12, 2023 | C11 

BY TIMOTHY FARRINGTON 

# LIKE HORSE breeders and Hollywood studio executives, the early Greeks saw the world mostly as combinations and adaptations of past hits. Hesiod’s “Theogony,” for example, pitched Okeanos—the river that sup-posedly ran round the rim of the world—as “Earth meets Sky,” and imagined Sleep, Death and Blame as spinoffs of dark Night. In this epic creation story, written around the eighth century B.C., the familiar Olympian gods, such as Zeus and Athena, are knit together with Titans, nymphs and half-personified forces (Desire, Deceit) into a giant family tree. The personal isn’t politi-cal, it’s cosmic. Then, around 600 B.C., on the west-ern coast of what is now Turkey, emerged the first in a chain of thinkers who sought to explain the universe in nonmythical terms. Among these “pre-Socratics” or “phusikoi ” (“natural-ists”), as Aristotle called them, we can trace the first stirrings of systematic philosophy and science. They consid-ered existence as a unified whole, ad-dressing metaphysics as well as phys-ics: How was the world made, and how many different ingredients did it take? What is essential and what is acciden-tal? Can things be created or de-stroyed? The spark for this intellectual revolution isn’t clear, but in “How to Be: Life Lessons From the Early Greeks,” Adam Nicolson offers a cir-cumstantial theory: what he calls the “harbor mind,” the openness and flexi-bility cultivated by life in a port city. Assigning such importance to place is a characteristic move for Mr. Nicol-son, an elegant and erudite British writer who grew up partly in his grandparents’ castle, Sissinghurst, and as a teenager was given a set of Scottish islands. “Places give access to minds,” he believes, and his earlier books have charted the cultural, his-toric and scientific contours of the landscapes that nourished the trans-lators of the King James Bible; Cole-ridge and Wordsworth; the Blooms-bury group; and him. His “Why Homer Matters” (2014), which is far more enjoyable than its oat-bran title, argues that Homer’s “poetry embod-ies the air of incorporated time, as rounded as something that for centu-ries has rolled back and forth on the stony beaches of Greece . . . where the Homeric ghosts can still be heard.” With “How to Be” he has set him-self a difficult task. The pre-Socratics survive only in short fragments: a few on scraps of papyrus, the rest quoted or paraphrased by later authors. The texts are abstract and often difficult. But Mr. Nicolson does his best to argue for their continuing influence. He also evokes the conditions—if not the definite causes—of the revolution that began “on the mobile edges” of the Greek world, among “merchants in ideas, people from communities in which exchange was the medium of significance.” Before getting to Miletus, the port that was home to the earliest pre-Socratics, Mr. Nicolson takes a run-ning start with a chapter on the “Od-yssey.” It is the “first poem to describe the world we know,” he writes, set in a “society more about cleverness than about heroism and dominance.” Odysseus’ wiles, Penel-ope’s time-wasting tricks to fend off her crowd of suitors: These are the sorts of things “we”—Mr. Nicolson seems to be thinking of worldly resi-dents of liberal democracies—relate to, unlike the antics of the honor-bound hotheads in the “Iliad.” Digging into the academic literature, he adopts a relatively late date (the mid-seventh century B.C.) for the point at which the text of the “Odyssey” was fixed. This allows him to interpret the work in the light of that era’s enrich-ing, destabilizing surge of trade between Greece and the proverbially luxurious lands to the east. This influx, he suggests, helped overturn traditional certainties, and the “Odys-sey,” with its emphasis on the need to improvise rather than rely on settled wisdom, “marks the beginning of the autonomous self.” Thales, the first of the pre-Socrat-ics, held that the world is, or was created from, water (the Greek text isn’t clear). Miletus, his hometown, is today a dusty burg. Over the centu-ries, its site has crept along the banks of the River Maeander to outpace accumulating silt. Mr. Nicolson thinks this process may have inspired Thales’ focus on water’s protean power: “If you can accept that all is water, you can begin to think beyond the ordinariness of things,” he writes. The beauty and centrality of the liquid is “Thales’ great gift to us.” (The author, it’s worth noting, is an avid sailor who had the idea for this book while diving in an Aegean har-bor to disentangle his boat’s anchor.) In Thales’ work, Mr. Nicolson iden-tifies a “kind of invitation to mental freedom,” but in his persona the au-thor sees reason to doubt the utility of philosophy. Thales is the prototype of the absent-minded professor, famously clever and famously foolish. An ancient anecdote had him staring so hard at the heavens that he fell into a well, drawing laughter from a passing Thracian servant girl. Thrace, a region spanning parts of modern Bulgaria, Turkey and Greece, was a prime source of slaves. Mr. Nicolson pauses to consider this seemingly trivial comic scene as both a reminder of the stark centrality of slavery to Greek society and a disquieting com-ment on the value of airy speculation. Is philosophy a tainted luxury made possible by exploitation? But Mr. Nicolson’s winning enthu-siasm barely flickers in the face of such doubts, as he examines how subsequent writers contributed to his twin ideals of adventurous self-realization and harmonious integra-tion. What about Anaximenes of Mile-tus, who considered the essential component of the universe to be air? “We are as the world is,” Mr. Nicolson concludes. “The air enters us when we are born and leaves us as we die.” Sappho, the great poet of private pas-sion? She is a “celebrant of ecstatic jointness” who “paraded multiplicity.” Sappho’s pluralist worldview “could not have emerged from an autocratic state,” Mr. Nicolson insists. She’s not only the one whose work he enlists to vindicate urban(e) life. Her-aclitus, the cryptic sage who sug-gested that ever-changing fire was common to all things? This dynamic view of life, Mr. Nicolson writes, held that “like a harbor city, reality embraced change.” Empedocles’ work, meanwhile, tells us that “in any city, love and strife must live within each other’s embrace,” and Parmenides bids us to live in both “the washed spirit of intellectual perfection” and the “burning, brilliant, beautifying bazaar of life on earth.” True enough, but it’s easy to see almost any place as motivation for such ideas—change is everywhere—and down at the docks you’ll find as many cautious old salts as daring adventurers. “The core of being is interplay, and its give and take of quick and still,” Mr. Nicolson wrote in “Life Between the Tides” (2021), in a phrase that could serve as a keynote for his works. Here he can seem too quick to find in the varied figures of pre-classical Greece a stable synthesis of opposites that flatters our own cosmopolitan preferences. But his exploration of the period is wonder-fully rich. He shows us ancient coins, scientific graphs, beautiful statuary, ruined temples and the “bony clarity of the coast.” He parses ancient texts and invokes the Alexandrian poet Constantine Cavafy, the mid-20th-century philosopher Karl Popper and the classicist Martin West, great expositor of Eastern influence on Greece. “How to Be” teaches many lessons, but most of all that we should savor the strange and stimu-lating legacy of this lesser-known era. 

Mr. Farrington is a former editor at Harper’s and the Journal.

A Strange Life: Selected Essays 

By Louisa May Alcott 

Notting Hill, 168 pages, $21.95 

BY MEGHAN COX GURDON 

# IN THE ANNALS of irresponsi-ble utopianism, few names stand out like that of Bronson Alcott. This dreamy 19th-century son of New England was a high-minded Transcendentalist and visionary teacher, and, as ahusband and father, a terrible pro-vider. Bronson’s propensity to run up debts and alienate supporters sank his young family into poverty. Had it not been for the guts and integrity of his wife, Abigail, the couple’s four girls would have starved. Had it not been for the busy and lucrative pen of their second daughter, Louisa May, the Alcott family might have disap-peared into the footnotes of history. As it is, they remain a subject of fasci-nation, for Louisa (1832-88) would produce not only the novels “Little Women” (1868) and “Little Men” (1871) but also short stories and bril-liant nonfiction sketches. Louisa was 10 when her father dragged the family off to live on sunshine and good vibes at a vegetar-ian commune that he co-founded with an exacting fellow traveler named Charles Lane. The experiment at Fruitlands, in rural Massachusetts, was almost comically pious and frugal. (“Witty onlookers called its residents ‘fruitcakes,’ ” biographer Liz Rosenberg has written.) For seven months, a tiny band of idealists dressed in brown linen dined on such spartan fare as unleavened bread, water and porridge. Such industry as anyone displayed was practiced by Abigail Alcott, who alone among the Fruitlands adults seems to have had some conscience about the welfare of its children. A painful anecdote illuminates the near-criminal idealism of her husband. In that exceptionally cold winter of 1843, a neighbor gave the communards a load of firewood. Rather than use it to warm his household, Bronson regifted the precious fuel to a local family with a baby. We can imagine his wife’s distress: She also had a baby. At the time there was little comedy in the Fruitlands ordeal; not so three decades later, when Louisa May Alcott turned those hard times into asparkling satirical essay, “Transcen-dental Wild Oats” (1873). This gem is one of the brightest in “A Strange Life,” a snug collection of Alcott’s writings edited by Ms. Rosenberg (whose young-adult biography of Alcott appeared in 2021) with a pref-ace by Jane Smiley. In Alcott’s account of communal life, she adopts a tone of mocking good humor that never tips into sour-ness or sarcasm. Appropriately, she styles her trusting father as Brother Lamb, her mother as Sister Hope (“unconverted but faithful to the end”) and the dictatorial Charles Lane as Brother Lion. It’s difficult to do jus-tice here to her tone and control of her subject, for “Transcendental Wild Oats” unrolls not as a series of scenes but as a single woven carpet of comi-cal ironies. Still, a few passages—on the virtuous working of Fruitlands soil without benefit of animals—give a glimpse of the delightful pattern. Alcott writes: “The band of broth-ers began by spading garden and field; but a few days of it lessened their ardor amazingly. Blistered hands icksburg. Alcott writes: “The sight of several stretchers, each with its legless, armless, or desperately wounded occupant, entering my ward, admonished me that I was there to work, not to wonder or weep; so I corked up my feelings, and returned to the path of duty.” Nurse Periwinkle keeps her sense of humor, though, even when she’s assigned the job of scrubbing soldiers free of the muck of war. Had the request been “to shave them all, or dance a hornpipe on the stove funnel,” Alcott confesses, “I should have been less staggered; but to scrub some dozen lords of creation at a moment’s notice, was really—really.” Girding her-self, she begins bathing a dirt-encrusted Irishman, and they both laugh. Soon she’s scrubbing away “like any tidy parent on a Saturday night,” as she puts it. “Some of them took the performance like sleepy children, lean-ing their tired heads against me as I worked, others looked grimly scandal-ized, and several of the roughest colored like bashful girls.” “A Strange Life” is a thorough pleasure. Modest in size and design— it’ll fit in a pocket—the book puts em-phasis where it belongs: on the vigor-ous prose of one of America’s most charming and memorable stylists. 

Mrs. Gurdon, a Journal contributor, is the author of “The Enchanted Hour: The Miraculous Power of Reading Aloud in the Age of Distraction.” 

In Alcott’s account of her father’s ill-fated experiment in communal life, she adopts a tone of mocking good humor. 

and aching backs suggested the expe-diency of permitting the use of cattle till the workers were better fitted for noble toil by a summer of the new life.” Later she observes: “The garden was planted with a generous supply of useful roots and herbs; but, as manure was not allowed to profane the virgin soil, few of these vegetable treasures ever came up.” The same arch spirit infuses “How I Went Out to Service” (1874), in which Alcott recounts her effort at 18 to help support her family. Hired as a lady’s companion, Louisa finds herself the drudge in a hilariously Gothic household dominated by a sanctimonious gourmand who styles himself “a frail reed.” The genius of the piece is the narrator’s imperfect self-knowledge; the older Alcott smiles at the innocence of her youn-ger self while leaving it to other characters to reveal the full preda-tory nature of her employer. The longest entry in “A Strange Life” is an essay about Alcott’s time as a volunteer nurse in the Civil War. As Ms. Rosenberg explains in an introduction, “Hospital Sketches” pro-vided a rare account of the war from a nurse’s perspective, and its 1863 publication made Alcott’s name. “Hospital Sketches” has a looser feel than the book’s other elements, which creates a curious sense of closeness; it’s as though Alcott were writing us a letter rather than offering a polished, impersonal account. The essay plays for comedy at the start, as the author, soon to be “Nurse Periwinkle,” flies about the house getting her things together. Thence follows a colorful travelogue as she journeys by train from Boston to Washington. Her story gains gravity when horse-drawn ambu-lances pull up to the hospital and dis-gorge men and boys left “wrecks of humanity” after the Battle of Freder-

# Utopia and Other Sites Of Note 

P2JW315000-4-C01100-1--------XA C12 | Saturday/Sunday, November 11 - 12, 2023 * * * * THE WALL STREET JOURNAL. 

BY JOANNE KAUFMAN 

# FIRST, THERE’S its sheer heft. At nearly 1,000 pages, Barbra Streisand’s memoir, “My Name Is Barbra,” is longer than the combined recollections of Prince Harry (“Spare”), Britney Spears (“The Woman in Me”) and Henry Winkler (“Being Henry”). Hello, gorge us. Then there’s its breadth. Granted, Ms. Streisand, now 81, is the enduring pop voice of her generation, not to mention a Broadway and Hollywood star, movie director, producer and composer, and political and social activist. She has met and worked with many famous people, befriended some of them, campaigned for a few (notably Bill and Hillary Clinton), and thus has lots of ground to cover. A winner of Emmys, Grammys, Oscars and a Tony, Ms. Streisand is a woman of many talents. Curating memories of the way she was—well, that isn’t one of them. Born Barbara, she grew up in the Brooklyn projects with an emotionally distant mother, a nasty stepfather, a determination to become an actress and a persistent ringing in her ears. Years later it was diagnosed as tinni-tus, a condition that continues to plague her. “It’s enough to drive you crazy. . . . That’s probably one reason why I need to keep busy all the time. I have to take my mind off it to try to distract myself. . . . I long for silence,” she writes. This plaintive observation would have packed a greater punch had it not been buried in an avalanche of minutiae about Ms. Streisand’s grade-school crushes, her pastries of choice at the local bakery, her ideal bagel toppings, her preferred beverage at the drugstore and her favorite treats at the movie theater. Still, she has an unbeatable description of Good &Plenty, cylinders of licorice coated in a pink and white candy shell. They were, she writes, “like eating jewelry.” Ms. Streisand finds a nice groove in her account of her first great suc-cess: a gig, in 1960, at the Greenwich Village nightclub the Bon Soir—a two-week booking that was extended and extended. She was making a name for herself in more ways than one. Never fond of Barbara, she’d had a eureka moment when considering the sign that would advertise her appearance at the club: “It occurred to me that I 

# BOOKS 

‘I arrived in Hollywood without having my nose fixed, my teeth capped or my named changed.’ —BARBRA STREISAND 

My Name Is Barbra 

By Barbra Streisand 

Viking, 992 pages, $47 

could just take out the middle a . . . . That was different and unique.” But the chapters that focus on Ms. Streisand’s early years are a template for the rest of the book. There may be gold there, but readers will have to pan diligently. She expends 30 pages on “The Way We Were,” the 1973 film in which she starred with Robert Red-ford. She chafes at camera angles, at the injudicious position of a hanky during a key moment, at dialogue that was excised, motivation that she thought was fuzzy and scenes that should never have been cut. Readers may be forgiven for wondering if somehow Ms. Streisand was confusing this mediocre romantic drama with “Citizen Kane.” Meanwhile, “Yentl” (1983), an ad-aptation of the Isaac Bashevis Singer short story “Yentl the Yeshiva Boy,” gets a Talmudic 100 pages. Ms. Strei-sand, a first-time director, also played the title role. Then again, it does sound like quite an eventful shoot. We learn that Ms. Streisand’s co-star, Mandy Patinkin, expected to have an affair with her. Or so the author claims. And we learn, rather less interestingly, that it was hard to shoot the climax of a song on a roll-ing ship just as the sun was setting. “The Prince of Tides” (1991), Ms. Streisand’s second movie as both star (she plays a psychiatrist) and director, goes on for 70 pages and features a good bit of not-always-on-point and less-than-profound free-lance analysis. “I think some people become therapists because they have problems themselves and they’re trying to find answers.” Perhaps be-lieving that digression is the better part of valor, Ms. Streisand fre-quently goes off on such tangents in “My Name Is Barbra,” only to return to the subject at hand: “back to the album”; “back to the Oscars”; “back again to 1970.” Ms. Streisand careers between ir-ritating disingenuousness—“I don’t find myself that fascinating,” she writes—and surprising self-aware-ness. “I had never looked for jobs in the chorus,” she notes. “Frankly, I don’t think it even occurred to me. Working my way up slowly didn’t figure into my plan.” She takes due note of her negativ-ity, her need for control, her ease at cutting people out of her life when they have disappointed her and her need to question everything. “I must have been very annoying to have around.” Bob Merrill, the “Funny Girl” lyricist, likely shared that view. “Isn’t it people who don’t need people who are the luckiest people in the world?” his young leading lady asked him. Anthony Newley, Pierre Trudeau, Andre Agassi and Omar Sharif were among those who looked beyond Ms. Streisand’s self-declared deficiencies. Her recollections of these romances are candid but respectful. And now it can be told: Clint Eastwood once com-plimented her ears, but that’s as far as things got. For 40 years, Ms. Streisand says, editors, including Jacqueline Onassis, asked her to write an autobiography. She steadfastly declined because of her desire to live in the present rather than dwell in the past. It should come as a surprise to exactly no one that, having finally acceded, Ms. Streisand charged full-bore into yesteryear. In so doing, she failed to remember her reaction to the overemoting Mr. Patinkin in the early days of the “Yentl” shoot: Sometimes less is more. There are, invariably, four ex-amples when two would do the job. No beading on a dress goes unat-tended. How nice to read Katharine Hepburn’s warm letter of congratula-tions to Ms. Streisand when the two of them shared the best-actress Oscar in 1969. But is it really necessary for Ms. Streisand to include her reply? “My Name Is Barbra” doesn’t have an index, so there are no shortcuts for impatient readers. If, for exam-ple, fans in Detroit wonder how Ms. Streisand felt about her 1961 engage-ment at the Caucus Club, a famed lo-cal boîte, they’ll have to start from page 1 and hope for the best. (Check pages 65-68.) It’s a lot to absorb. The audio version of the book, recorded by Ms. Streisand and featuring music clips and dozens of supposedly ex-temporaneous asides, runs for more than 48 hours. Even her most de-voted followers will be crying uncle or, more to the point, Yentl. 

Ms. Kaufman writes about art and culture for the Journal. 

# A Star Is Boring 

> BILL EPPRIDGE/THE LIFE PICTURE COLLECTION/SHUTTERSTOCK

Streisand spends dozens of pages on a single film she starred in. But ‘The Way We Were’ is hardly ‘Citizen Kane.’ 

An Immense World 

By Ed Yong (2022) 

# 1As a kid I had a beloved teddy bear we kept in a glass jar (due to a dust allergy and possibly the universe’s eagerness to introduce me to the joys of meta-phor), so I’m no stranger to anthro-pomorphization. We’re predisposed to think that other beings—human and nonhumans alike—are looking out with a perspective close to our own. Not so. It turns out that the sensory perceptions of other animals are often wildly different from our own. For example, we learn in Ed Yong’s “An Immense World” that the mantis shrimp possesses four times as many color receptors as humans, yet ecologists think they only per-ceive 12 colors. Go figure. The point is: We barely understand the tiniest sliver of what it’s like to be in some-one or something else’s, let’s say, shoes. We can understand a bit more if we try really hard and break out the scientific equipment. But it will always be important to remember that the majority of another being’s experience is theirs and theirs alone. 

Travels With Charley 

By John Steinbeck (1962) 

# 2I’ve spent a good chunk of my life traveling across the U.S. in tour buses. John Steinbeck’s account of driving from New York to California and back again with his dog Charley has given me a sense of kinship while on the road. It’s nice, even when you’re traveling with people you love, to be reminded that others have been exploring and drawing lessons from this land for centuries. Steinbeck makes it both timely and timeless, with lines like, “once a journey is designed, equipped, and put in process, a new factor enters and takes over. A trip, a safari, an explo-ration, is an entity, different from all other journeys. It has personality, temperament, individuality, unique-ness. A journey is a person in itself; no two are alike. And all plans, safe-guards, policing, and coercion are fruitless. We find after years of struggle that we do not take a trip; a trip takes us. Tour masters, sched-ules, reservations, brass-bound and inevitable, dash themselves to wreckage on the personality of the trip. Only when this is recognized can the blown-in-the glass bum relax and go along with it.” 

The Philosophy of Modern Song 

By Bob Dylan (2022) 

# 3The only thing I love as much as Bob Dylan’s music is Bob Dylan’s love of other people’s music. Whether it’s his “Theme Time Radio Hour” that aired from 2006 to 2009, or “The Philoso-phy of Modern Song,” I always feel like I have a lot to gain from learn-ing what he thinks about songs. Even when he’s being inscrutable, which—let’s face it—happens a lot in this book. “Today, the medium con-bus moaned and wailed through the pitch-black French countryside and I cowered in my bunk, as yet unaf-flicted, I thought about this book, which chronicles Ernest Shackleton’s attempted and ultimately disastrous journey to the South Pole beginning in 1914. Remembering the depriva-tions and hopelessness of Shackleton and his crew’s experience, locked in ice for more than 13 months, gave me comfort. Somehow, the entire group survived. Oh, and Shackleton made it home, too. 

Pilgrim at Tinker Creek 

By Annie Dillard (1974) 

# 5More than any other book I’ve ever read, “Pilgrim at Tinker Creek” helps me understand how to pay attention, which I think is maybe the most important skill or muscle I have as an artist and a human. Annie Dillard’s story is set in Virginia’s Roanoke Valley, where she spent all of a year exploring the nat-ural world around her. She turns the comings and goings of ants and bee-tles into riveting poetry. Or, I guess I should say, she reveals the riveting poetry of ants and beetles. It’s really both—the beauty in “Tinker Creek” is the collaboration between Ms. Dillard and our world through the creatures and landscapes she encountered that year. When I hike—but also every day, in any con-text—I aspire to her level of interest in and care for her surroundings, both inanimate and living.

Twister | by Patrick Berry 

Answers fit into this grid in two ways, Rows and Twists. Rows contain two answers clued in order; in Row 7, the black square is the dividing point between answers. Each set of Twist answers fills one of the grid’s long shaded or unshaded paths, starting at one corner and spiraling inward. The answers within each set are clued in order, but you must determine which set goes in which path. At least two squares in each path must be skipped in order to make the Twist answers fit. Skipped squares can appear in the middle of a Twist answer or between two Twist answers. The resulting empty square will be filled by the crossing Row. When the puzzle is complete, the letters in the skipped squares will spell some nice words (reading left to right) and some tiresome words (reading top to bottom).

Cell Blocks 

Divide the grid into square or rectangular blocks, each containing one digit only. Every block must contain the number of cells indicated by the digit inside it. 

Cell Blocks Killer Sudoku Level 2 Suko Killer Sudoku Level 3 

As with standard Sudoku, fill the grid so that every column, every row and every 3x3 box contains the digits 1 to 9. Each set of cells joined by dotted lines must add up to the target number in its top-left corner. Within each set of cells joined by dotted lines, a digit cannot be repeated. 

Suko 

Place the numbers 1 to 9 in the spaces so that the number in each circle is equal to the sum of the four surrounding spaces, and each color total is correct.

P2JW315000-4-C01300-1--------XA C14 | Saturday/Sunday, November 11 - 12, 2023 * * * * * THE WALL STREET JOURNAL. 

IN 1893, CORNETIST W.C. HANDY, 

broke and hungry in St. Louis, had reached a low point in his life. When he encountered “a woman whose pain seemed even greater” than his, he heard her mutter, “Ma man’s got a heart like a rock cast in de sea.” In 1914, now a successful Memphis bandleader, Handy drew on her words, he said, as well as on his imagination and his familiarity with African-American folk culture to write “St. Louis Blues.” “The wail of a lovesick woman for her lost man,” as Handy called it, the piece went on to become one of the most familiar, widely performed Ameri-can songs of all time. Its opening lines became part of the sound-scape: 

I hate to see the eve-ning sun go down, Hate to see the eve-nin’ sun go down. ’Cause my baby, he done left this town. 

In respectable households in late 19th-century America, music such as the blues and ragtime were con-sidered disreputable. The son and grandson of preachers who frowned upon secular music, Handy—who was born 150 years ago on Nov. 16, 1873, in Florence, Ala.—had studied classical music and conceded that he “took up with low forms hesi-tantly.” 

# At 20 years old, golf phenom Rose Zhang has a reputation for poise under pres-sure—for being calm and disciplined in her approach to the game and her expectations about herself. Although known for breaking records and dominating the competition as an amateur, in June when she became the first woman in more than 70 years to win her professional golf debut, she expressed surprise: “I honestly didn’t even expect to make the cut.” After tying for third in the May-bank Championship in Kuala Lum-pur, Malaysia, in late October, she said she just feels “really lucky,” adding: “I still have lots to learn.” Under that demeanor is a fierce drive. “Even though I’m considered pretty mellow, pretty chill, I defi-nitely do have a very competitive spirit,” Zhang says over video from Malaysia just before the Maybank tournament. She has admitted that as a rising star on golf’s junior cir-cuit in Southern California, she kept her 25 or so gold medals but threw away up to 20 silver ones, because who cares about second place? “I think that fight in me, that grind, has definitely allowed me to be where I am now,” she says. As she closes out her impressive first season on the Ladies Profes-sional Golf Association (LPGA) tour, which ends with the CME Group Tour Championship in Naples, Fla., next week, Zhang is often described in hopeful, breathless terms as the Tiger Woods of women’s golf. She has a duly glittery record, having joined the tour after becoming the most decorated female amateur in golf history. She bagged two con-secutive NCAA individual titles, an unprecedented feat. She even bested Woods’s record at Stanford University—where she is still a stu-dent—prevailing in 12 of 20 college tournaments, compared with his 11 in 26. Many now hail her as the star the tour needs to juice its TV rat-ings, which should yield bigger prize purses and better sponsorship deals for all women in the sport. This sounds like a lot of pressure for a player who is still juggling homework. “There’s a time and place to take certain opportunities when they are in front of you, and I was in a really good spot,” she says of her decision to join the LPGA tour midseason in June, on the heels of winning the Augusta National Women’s Amateur and her second NCAA title. Having spent a record 141 weeks as the top ama-teur in the world, and with backing from Adidas, Callaway, Delta, Rolex and others, she says she was ready to level up: “If I’m on that streak, why not take it?” When Zhang returns to Stanford in January, she will have over $1.2 million in prize money in her back pocket from her 6-month world tour. Although Woods’s success as a player nudged him to drop out of first-generation immigrants, but Zhang felt at home in the sport. A natural athlete, she says she always loved being outside and playing anything with a ball, but never cared for running or fast-reacting “quick-twitch stuff.” Golf, with its quiet intensity and technical preci-sion, applied her natural gifts and appealed to her competitive edge. She is left-handed but competes with right-handed clubs because those are what she used as a kid. “I didn’t even know there were left-handed golf clubs when I first started,” she says. Zhang is often described as a prodigy. Anne Walker, her coach at Stanford, has called her “the Mo-zart of golf, the Van Gogh of golf,” explaining that her genius for the game cannot be taught. Zhang has honed her mechanics to dependably hit the ball on the center of the club face—a bedrock goal of every golfer but elusive even to many pros. “In a game, there are a lot of factors that are out of your control, that you can’t really do anything about,” she says. “So instead of fo-cusing on the result, I’ve always thought about the process, the preparation, the practice.” The life of a professional is full of lessons, some learned the hard way. Although Zhang is no stranger to jet lag, she says she is now ad-justing to a life defined by disori-ented fatigue: “It’s more of a life-style, not just something to get used to.” She had a strong start to her season but her performance slipped before rebounding at May-bank with a new caddie. “I feel like I’m slowly navigating what it’s like to have certain dynamics and rela-tionships while I’m on tour, with other players, with caddies, with my dad, with my inner circle, with my team, with agents, with coaches,” she says. “You know, there’s a lot that goes on behind the scene in order to play good golf.” Zhang has said that her strength is not necessarily in being the best player on the course but the most consistent, making the fewest mis-takes. She describes herself as “in tune” with what her body is doing, able to tweak her form to get her swing just right. In the quiet mo-ments before a putt, when all eyes are on her, she says she enters what feels like a “meditative mind-set”: “I’m always thinking about how to better myself and how to commit to every single shot.”

college, Zhang insists that there is value in working toward her com-munications degree. She explains that she could have gone pro sooner—“I knew my game was there in high school”—but sensed that a proper college experience would help her grow and appreciate who she is away from golf. “To get to an elite level, you have to make a lot of sacrifices, you have to really dedicate yourself to your passion and the game, but in the midst of that a lot of people burn out,” Zhang says. She adds that time on a top college campus, surrounded by other impressive young people with different inter-ests and ambitions, keeps her feel-ing “rooted and grounded as a per-son outside of the sport.” She understands that being a top golfer and maintaining her 3.87 grade-point average will be tricky, but she is inspired by the example of Mi-chelle Wie West, a Stanford alum and recently retired golf pro who played professionally while attend-ing the school, and graduated: “I figured if someone else did it be-fore me, why can’t I?” Zhang was 9 when she picked up one of her dad’s golf clubs for the first time in their backyard and thought, “Oh, this seems like fun.” Her father, who moved from She-nyang, China, to California with her mom and older brother in 2001, liked the look of her swing and sug-gested some lessons. “I just fell in love with it,” she says. She prac-ticed constantly, hitting hundreds of balls with her father in a field near their home in Irvine. Within months, she won her first tourna-ment. # REVIEW 

> DAVID GOTHARD

filmed in 1956, two years before Handy’s death, the camera captures a touching moment when Louis Armstrong performs “St. Louis Blues” in an overblown orchestral arrangement conducted by Leonard Bernstein, while in the front row the aged, now-blind composer listens transfixed to his signature composi-tion, dabbing tears from his eyes. The song’s many ideas, compel-ling contrast, evocative lyrics and plaintive mood—along with Handy’s energetic marketing—combined to make “St. Louis Blues” the most significant and successful blues of all time. 

Mr. Hasse is curator emeritus of American music at the Smithso-nian’s National Museum of Ameri-can History. His books include “Be-yond Category: The Life and Genius of Duke Ellington” (Da Capo) and “Discover Jazz” (Pearson). 

MASTERPIECE | ‘ST. LOUIS BLUES’ (1914), BY W.C. HANDY 

# A Singular Song of Woe 

As he traveled the South as an itinerant musician, Handy listened closely to the mélange of melodies in the air, began notating songs, and adapted some for publication. He titled his 1941 autobiography “Father of the Blues,” but he did not invent them. Instead, he was the first to notate, arrange, publish and popularize the idiom that pre-viously existed only in aural tradi-tion. He transmuted a fleeting form into something permanent. His songs primed the public’s ears for the down-home, country blues that record companies began issuing in the 1920s. The blues would become a mighty river that flowed through most styles of American music. As a form, its three-line lyric and 12 bars of music washed into country mu-sic, jazz, rock ’n’ roll and soul. Handy gave the world a singular song: fresh, inspired and infectious. With its three themes, “St. Louis Blues” is much richer in musical form and contrast than expected. The first and third themes, both 12-bar blues, pique interest by offering breaks, and the final section in-cludes some boogie-woogie bass figures. The remarkable second theme, a 16-bar strain, is in a minor key and in tango rhythm, a dance step that was wildly popular in 1914. The three different melodies, with their seemingly bent (or “blue”) notes, are singable and memorable. 

BY JOHN EDWARD HASSE With their slang and vivid imagery, the lyrics are catchy. While the tone of “St. Louis Blues” is lamenting, the act of singing such a blues provides ca-tharsis for the singer and, by ex-tension, the audience. Handy promoted “St. Louis Blues” as both composer and pub-lisher. In the 1920s, more than 60 jazz recordings were made of it, and the number increased in the 1930s. Ultimately, whether as a vo-cal or instrumental, “St. Louis Blues” became the second most re-corded American song (“Star Dust,” by Hoagy Carmichael and Mitchell Parish, is first), with versions by artists as varied as Pete Seeger, Chuck Berry, Angela Brown, George Thorogood, Paul Robeson, Duke El-lington, Louis Armstrong (who made it a signature tune), Sun Ra and Stevie Wonder with Herbie Hancock. As of 2023, more than 2,200 recordings have been made in the jazz tradition alone and hun-dreds in other genres. The piece, poet Langston Hughes wrote in 1941, “is sung more than any other song on the air waves, is known in Shanghai and Buenos Ai-res, Paris and Berlin—in fact, is heard so often in Europe that a great many Europeans think it must be the Ameri-can National Anthem.” Dur-ing World War II, when the Nazis denounced jazz, musicians evaded the authorities by masking the song as “La Tristesse de Saint Louis” (“The Sadness of Saint Louis”), “Das Lied vom Heiligen Ludwig” (“The Song of Saint Louis”) or the Czech protest “The Song of Rese-tová Lhota.” The song has taken on many guises. In 1940 pianist Earl “Fatha” Hines recorded it as “Boogie Woo-gie on St. Louis Blues,” in 1944 Glenn Miller’s band transformed it into “St. Louis Blues March,” and in 1955 Perez Prado made it into a mambo. The original can be heard in at least 40 motion pictures, most notably the 16-minute “St. Louis Blues” of 1929, in which Bessie Smith, in her only screen appear-ance, sings with affective power and majestic sorrow. In a scene from the documentary “Satchmo the Great” that was

To help you cut to the chase, we pored over mountains of metrics at hundreds of North American resorts, from skiable acres and snowfall to hot-tub density and the concentra-tion of craft breweries. After setting parame-ters to exclude resorts that are too small or just not snowy enough, we used raw data from weather and review aggregator OnTheSnow to create three subcategories: snow, terrain and après. We then combined those results with data focused on categories of skiers to yield even more tailored lists, with families, ambi-tious experts or regional loyalists in mind. The winners showcase just how varied North American skiing has become. Though a famed Canadian megaresort snagged the top spot in our overall ranking, there’s no one-size-fits-all option. Our trail analysis points beginners to a historic Colorado resort, while East Coasters seeking prime conditions closer to home might want to plot their path to the New England peak that topped the sub-rank-ing for snowfall. # STYLE & FASHION 

# SOME MEN aspire to style. Others aim no higher than merely being clothed, thus avoiding a chill, a demotion or a short, vivid prison term on charges of indecency. Sooner or later, style-minded sorts grapple with the concept of “layer-ing,” asking simple questions like, “Can one wear a V-neck sweater over a V-neck T-shirt and not appear sad and inept?” (No.) For more complex questions such as, “Can one wear 14 sweaters at once, plus a tie, a cra-vat and a woolly beret without collapsing un-der the weight?” dashing men of the 1970s turned to “Dressing Right,” a now-cult book by former GQ columnist Charles Hix. This collectible style guide, first published in 1978, offered tips on achieving a look sometimes called “Heavy Duty Ivy.” In an un-precedented way, this approach layered classic preppy clothes with more formal elements and outdoor gear. Much of the book’s wisdom still ap-plies today: Daiki Suzuki, the designer behind the brand Engineered Garments, has credited it with shaping his spring 2024 menswear collection. But some of the book’s more ambitious outfits appear bonkers in retrospect. Here, a few of its hits and messy misses. 

This classic 1970s men’s style guide offers timeless layering tips—except when it lurches into lunacy 

BY DALE HRABI AND JAMIE WATERS 

# Excessively Dressed? 

OPEN SEASON | This guy’s layering feels easy and unforced because the pieces are left unbuttoned. Closing yourself up in sealed layers adds Michelin-Man bulk. A belt-defined waist also averts an overly swaddled look and an actually functional scarf—substantial, not wispy— earns its place in the mix. Bed-head hair underlines the unfussiness.

# ANYONE who lived through the 1990s (or has watched Apple TV+’s docuseries “The Super Models”) no doubt remem-bers the decade’s taste for brown makeup. To emulate era-defining faces such as model Linda Evangelista and R&B star Brandy—both particularly partial to mo-cha-hue lips—women em-braced earthy maquillage en masse. Today, amid an am-ber wave of ’90s-style nos-talgia, makeup brands are re-visiting such tones, but the look du jour is an evolution, not a reproduction. Fans of “latte makeup,” as it’s been dubbed, include ce-lebrities such as models Hai-ley Bieber and Lori Harvey, and actress Shay Mitchell— as well as legions of Gen-Z TikTok users. The look kicks the original ’90s trend up a notch with added dimension and luminosity. “Neutral tones and monochromatic shades are elevated and so-phisticated,” said New York makeup artist Delina Medhin of fall’s coffeehouse palette. 

BY JAYME CYK 

THROWBACK LATTE Actress and musician Brandy in her signature brown lips in 1998. 

‘Latte makeup’ is percolating. How to get the ’90s-inspired look. 

# Beauty’s New Brew 

For a seamless finish, don’t be afraid to use the same product, like Danessa Myricks Colorfix Pigment, on your eyes, lips and cheeks, Medhin instructed. That said, your latte should never be one-note. Don’t forget to bring addi-tional dimensions to your skin with colors other than brown, Medhin said. “If you look at an image of Hailey Bieber, you can see she’s wearing a little bit of pink on the lips mixed with a brown-ish tone. She still has pink on the cheeks, too.” Adding in pinks, peaches or purples (whatever looks most natural on your skin), especially when it comes to blush and lips, makes latte makeup appear more natural, said Medhin. She advocates combining brown and pink lip pigments and defining your pout with a brown liner (like the Make Up For Ever pencil, below). “If you go too brown, it starts to look heavy and dated,” warned Dang, especially if you have a light complexion. Los Angeles producer Ilona Klaver, 37, also consid-ers today’s latte makeup more “elevated” than its ’90s forebear—even if it still achieves a moody result. “It [makes you] feel done-up in a neutral way, without look-ing over the top,” she said. In other words, without looking overly caffeinated. 

Latte makeup comes amid an amber wave of ’90s nostalgia. 

> FEDERICA DEL PROPOSTO (ILLUSTRATION); GETTY IMAGES (2)

BRONZE ARTIST Hailey Bieber confidently poses in latte makeup in Paris in September. 

“Plus, latte makeup has a sexy vibe.” Begin brewing your latte look with a healthy helping of moisturizer, advised Los Angeles makeup artist Carola Gonzalez. Then, apply a dewy-finish foundation in your go-to shade. From there, you’ll want to add warm hues that complement your color-ing. Darker-skin individuals should seek out makeup with orange undertones, suggested Gonzalez. “For medium skin tones, I would use golds, and for lighter skin tones, use something more champagne.” 

Heed that advice when se-lecting a bronzer (like the Chanel cream formula below) to warm up your canvas. For eyes that rival the Starbucks siren’s, experiment with nude and brown shad-ows (we like Fenty Beauty by Rihanna’s Eyeshadow Stick) and smudge as needed to avoid severe lines. “The ’90s [version] is more harsh lines, whereas modern latte makeup is glowy, blended and softened,” said Jessica Dang, 42, a New York well-ness entrepreneur who dis-covered the look on TikTok.

# STYLE & FASHION 

right? Wrong: So many peo-ple’s frames are ill-fitting, said Charlotte Jones, owner of Charlotte Jones Opticians in the Garment District. The nose-bridge or pads should sit properly on your schnoz; the bent end of the arm must hang on your ear. Your eyes should be as close as possible to centered in the lens, said Erik Sacher, co-founder of Soho’s Silver Lin-ing Opticians. Slightly above center is fine, he said. Frames shouldn’t exceed the width of your face to the degree that people looking through your lenses can see “a lot of air” on either side of your head, said Sacher. Beware: Overly wide glasses can make eyes appear close together or crossed, said George Lee, an optician and the store manager at Oliver Peoples in Midtown. Those with prescriptions stronger than about minus 4 should take extra care to avoid huge frames, said Sacher, since their lenses will be thicker and (among other things) their faces can look distorted. Though wire frames, women’s aviators and subtle men’s cat-eyes are especially trendy now, said Sacher, few styles are objectively uncool. (Except for rectangular frames as squished as slits, which should be illegal.) “Look at the whole pic-ture,” said Marilyn Blumen-gold, a sales associate at Moscot in the Lower East Side who tells me she’s been selling specs “longer than you have walked the earth.” Con-sider your facial features— but do so with a heap of salt. Very broadly speaking, said Adam Cassel, an optician and the manager at Studio Optix in Midtown, “If you have a square-shaped face, we want to soften that with rounder edges. If you have a rounder face, round glasses would em-phasize that, so we try to bal-ance things with angles. But I’m not going to say, ‘This is your face shape, and that means you can [only] wear this shape.’” He suggests get-ting two frames: a fun pair and a safer everyday option. Don’t fret too much about eyebrows, as long as you can’t spot a whole brow in-side the lens—an unforgiv-able sin, according to the pros. Better to focus on your eyes. To bring mine out, op-ticians told me to try glasses in similar colors to my irises. I tried not to take offense when they kept handing me swamp-green frames. Look for hues that offer “a nice amount of contrast” against your skin, said Cassel. Softer, faded tones tend to comple-ment paler skin, while bolder, saturated hues shine on darker complexions, said Jones. Soyoung Lee, chief curator at the Harvard Art Museums in Cambridge, Mass., went ul-tra-bright with her frames— lime-green cat-eyes by Alain Mikli. “They have a bit of funk,” said Lee, 52. “To me, this color [projects] fun and a sense of humor.” She’s yet to tire of them after five years,

Glasses can define your style. But what makes a great, flattering pair of frames? 

# Looking for the Ones 

# FOR THE PAST 

six years a pair of round, gold glasses has shown me the world. But the lenses have grown so scratched and cloudy that it feels like I’m peering through a fog. The other morning, I tried to pay for a coffee with my driver’s license. Enough was enough. I needed fresh frames, fast. Still, I wasn’t about to choose my replacements willy-nilly. A style metamor-phosis was at stake. Since glasses so prominently guard the windows to our souls, they arguably define our look and vibe more than any other item. Skimp on sweat-ers and sneakers if you must, but don’t settle for ho-hum frames. New specs “redefine who you are,” said Julie Ragolia, a fashion editor and stylist based between Paris and New York. “You have to think about, ‘What will my new face look like?’” Ask yourself other search-ing questions too: Do I want my glasses to be a support-ing act or a scene-stealer? How big is too big? And if I fall for a pair of fun, chunky frames, am I legally bound to attend gallery openings and use words like “oeuvre”? Cue weeks of traipsing to optical stores across Man-hattan—and quizzing their resident experts—in search of “the Ones.” I popped avia-tors, cat-eyes and Stanley Tucci’s go-to black Moscot frames on my beak; ringed my peepers with marble, calf leather and titanium. The number one lesson I picked up? Ensure your glasses fit you. No duh, 

BY JAMIE WATERS 

‘What frames are just different enough to get compliments?’ 

perhaps because the smallish frame tempers the zesty hue. Eugene Pierce, who works in risk management at UBS in New York, takes a sizable risk with his glasses, favoring Fanta-orange frames by Bel-gian brand Theo Eyewear. The 55-year-old teams them with Savile Row suits and Charvet ties. “Life is too short to wear boring glasses,” he said. Other stylish folks choose frames with subtler points of distinction. Stewart Bean, 36, a New York graphic designer, appreciates the raw-looking matteness of his tortoiseshell pair by Anne & Valentin, while Nicole Fearon, 33, a medical-office manager in Austin, picked her gold Warby Parkers for their gently octagonal shape. “This will probably sound vain,” she said, “but I think about it as: ‘What frames are just dif-ferent enough for somebody to compliment them?’” I’m with her. I can’t pull off sherbet-colored glasses, but I want compliments. So I sought out designs in just-left-of-center shapes and shades, finding a sweet spot in the $300-$600 category. I zeroed in on frames in grays, greens and honeys, or with contrasting arms and bridges, and contemplated getting gradient-tinted lenses, like the stylist Ragolia does. Blumengold told me to seek input from one, and only one, person I trust when making a decision. Any extra voices muddy things. (And when sending that trusted person references, ask the salesperson to snap a pic. Never take selfies— they magnify glasses, making you appear bug-eyed.) I duly installed my part-ner as the Simon Cowell for my glasses’ auditions. He wasted no time in making pronouncements. According to him, in various frames, I resembled: a “serial killer” (silver aviators); a “big bug” (thick green frames); and, in a wide pair with a ramrod-straight line across the tops of the lenses and nose, a “car dashboard.” Then I found what I’d been looking for. This pair, a $395 acetate design by Japanese brand Eyevan, calmly assert their presence with a semi-chunky, angular form, yet won’t loudly announce them-selves when I enter a room. When I tried them on, my partner, taking his new role to heart, declared, “You’re a posh Englishman in a board-room—by a fireplace.” Re-ally, though, I felt like my-self. Only sharper. In these smoky gray frames, my swampy eyes sing.

The most-common of-fender, according to several pros: frames so wide that they basically fall off your face. This doesn’t look cool; it makes your eyes look close together.

Also bad: When petite frames’ arms don’t hook around the ears.

D6 | Saturday/Sunday, November 11 - 12, 2023 * * * * THE WALL STREET JOURNAL. 

constant. When a recipe sim-ply hits all the right notes, any thought of altering it grinds to a halt for a good de-cade or so. My cranberry sauce is one such recipe. I’ve made the same one for at least a dozen years. It’s as simple as cranberries, Port, sugar and orange zest, and so deeply entwined with the thought of Thanksgiving for me that—as if by some edict— I never make it any other day of the year, even though I crave it routinely. It’s not about depriving myself or my family; it’s about nurturing ritual and the taste-memories inherent in it. This fall, though, I ended up with a 5-pound bag of deli-cious frozen cranberries from Fresh Meadows Farm in southeastern Massachusetts— far more than I need for Thanksgiving. So I decided to experiment, which is easy to do because cranberry sauce is more technique than recipe. Here’s what you need to know: For starters, fresh and fro-zen cranberries work equally well. What matters is the source. Choose cranberries free of pesticides, and always rinse them in abundant water, then pick through and discard any that are tight and shriv-eled or have burst. If I find a very pale one, I’ll toss it, as it likely isn’t ripe. Next, place the cranberries in a saucepan. They should come up the sides about a third of the way. You then want to cover the berries with whatever liquid you are using, whether cider, wine or juice. They should be just submerged rather than drowning. Now add your choice of sugar—white, brown or raw all work well, as does honey—and flavorings, such as spices, citrus zest or roots like ginger or turmeric. Bring the mixture to a sim-mer and continue to cook for approximately 20 minutes. The cranberries will pop and burst, and the sauce will thicken and glisten. Remove from heat and let the sauce come fully to room tempera-ture before storing it in a glass jar in the refrigerator until needed, up to one week. 

# MORE than perhaps any other meal of the year, Thanksgiv-ing dinner remains—for many of us, at least—pretty much a 

BY ALEKSANDRA CRAPANZANO 

> F. MARTIN RAMIN/THE WALL STREET JOURNAL, FOOD STYLING BY KIM RAMIN; ISTOCK (CRANBERRIES, CRÈME FRAÎCHE)

Go ahead and have a little fun with the classic Thanksgiving sauce. The effortless recipe lends itself to reinvention. And while you’re at it, why not get a dessert out of it, too? 

# Yes, You Cranberry 

Port Cranberry Sauce 

Total Time 25 minutes 

Serves 10 

2 pounds fresh cranberries, rinsed and picked over 1 (750 ml) bottle ruby Port 13

/4 cups sugar Zest of 1 orange (optional) 1. In a heavy bottomed saucepan over medium heat, combine all ingredi-ents and bring to a boil. When cranberries pop and start to burst, reduce heat and simmer until sauce has thickened and reached the consistency you like, 15-20 minutes. Let cool to room temperature. 

2. Serve immediately or re-frigerate up to 1 week. Bring back to room tem-perature before serving. 

Unfortunately, cranberry sauce doesn’t freeze well, as the texture disintegrates. How much to make, then? As a rule of thumb, 24 ounces of berries will feed a dozen guests. But why not make 2 pounds and have leftovers for turkey sandwiches? Or, make your go-to sauce plus an excit-ing experiment. Follow the ba-sic method laid out here and it’s a low-risk proposition. When I tried mixing the arils from a pomegranate into a bowl of cranberry sauce, those ruby-colored seed pods bestowed a welcome juicy freshness and a slightly tart bite to offset the sweetness in the sauce. The arils do not cook well, so they must be added cool, raw and last-min-ute, once the cranberry sauce has come to room tempera-ture or been chilled. Firm, cool arils wake up the palate, particularly pleasing when the rest of the meal is warm and rich. A dash of pomegranate molasses, meanwhile, can be added while the sauce cooks, for a slightly bittersweet com-plexity. A touch of rose water or a sprinkle of fresh or dried rose petals will add a pretty floral dimension. Pastry chef Toni Weiburg of Union Square Café in Man-hattan makes a crowd-pleas-ing cranberry sauce charged with all the warming spices we associate with autumn and its holidays. Orange juice and zest keep it lively, and vanilla and brown sugar round it off in a gentle balancing act. It’s the most kid-friendly of the three recipes included here, and an easy one to replicate over the winter months when dinner is roast chicken or lunchbox turkey sandwiches need a little cheerful color. If you make my Port cran-berry sauce, consider dou-bling the recipe to extend to a winter Pavlova or an Eton mess of meringue and whipped cream. Cranberry sauce makes a sensational stand-in for the more-typical summer strawberries. If I have leftover pie dough too, I’ll make a galette a day or two after Thanksgiving— though you can certainly serve yours the day of. The sauce makes a wonderful fill-ing, and the free-form nature of a galette lets you use as much or as little dough as you have. For best results, scoop vanilla ice cream directly onto a galette warm from the oven, creating rivers of swirling va-nilla in the jewel-red sauce and a slice of pure comfort.

Cranberry sauce is more of a technique than a recipe. Here’s what you need to know. 

# EATING & DRINKING Cranberry Sauce Galette 

Active Time 1hour Total Time 

51

/2 hours (in-cludes chilling) 

Serves 6

22

/3 cups (340 grams) all-purpose flour 1 tablespoon granulated sugar 

> 1

/4 teaspoon fine sea salt 1 cup plus 2 tablespoons (255 grams) cold unsalted butter 1 tablespoon apple cider vinegar 

> 1

/2 cup ice water 2 cups Port cranberry sauce (recipe below) 1 egg 2 teaspoons whole milk 1 tablespoon turbinado or raw sugar 

> 1

/2 cup crème fraiche 1 cup heavy cream 1. Place metal blade of a food processor in freezer for 20 minutes. 

2. With chilled blade in place, in food processor, combine flour, sugar and salt. Pulse to integrate. Cut butter into about 15 pieces, add and pulse 20 times. Com-bine vinegar and water. Add 1

/4 cup of this mixture to dough and pulse 7 times. Add remaining liquid and pulse 7 times. 

3. Transfer dough to a work surface and gather into a ball. Halve dough equally and roll each half into a ball. 

4. Wrap one ball in plastic wrap, then aluminum foil, and place in a sealable freezer bag. Transfer to freezer. (Dough will keep 10 weeks in freezer. Thaw overnight in refrigerator before using.) 

5. Place second dough ball on a sheet of parch-ment. With the heel of your hand, press dough into a small circle. Wrap in plastic and refrigerate at least 3 hours and up to 48. 

6. Remove dough from refrigerator, unwrap and let sit 5 minutes at room tempera-ture on a large sheet of parch-ment. Place a second large sheet of parch-ment on top of dough. With a rolling pin, roll dough into a cir-cle 1

/4 inch thick. 

7. Slide dough circle on its parchment onto a baking sheet. Strain and discard excess liquid from cranberry sauce. Fill dough circle with cranberry sauce, leaving a 11

/2 inch border all around. Fold bor-der up and over sauce and lightly press folds to-gether. Refriger-ate 30 minutes. Meanwhile, pre-heat oven to 425 degrees. 

8. In a small bowl, whisk together egg and milk. Brush galette bor-ders with egg wash. Sprinkle with turbinado sugar. Bake until crust is golden, 50 minutes. Serve warm or at room temperature. 

9. Use a stand mixer or electric beaters to whip creams together until billowy. Pass crème Chantilly at the table. 

HAVE IT YOUR WAY A delicious Port cranberry sauce not only suits the ceremonial bird; it also provides an easy filling for a festive galette.
