# EATING & DRINKING 

Total Time 20 minutes 

Serves 4

3 tablespoons olive oil, plus more for drizzling 4 ( 1

/2-inch-thick) slices sourdough bread 1 tablespoon red wine vinegar 

> 1

/2 teaspoon Dijon mustard Salt and freshly ground black pepper 10 ounces fresh goat cheese, cut into 1

/2-inch-thick rounds 2 heads radicchio or 1 head frisée, or a combination 3 tablespoons minced fresh dill 3 tablespoons minced chives, mint, tarragon and/or parsley 3 radishes, thinly sliced 1 tablespoon honey, to garnish (optional) 1. Preheat oven to 450 de-grees. Drizzle some olive oil over the sourdough, lightly coating both sides, and ar-range slices on a baking sheet. Toast on oven’s top rack until golden and crisp, about 10 minutes. 

2. Make the dressing: In a small bowl, whisk together vinegar and mustard. Slowly drizzle in 3 tablespoons olive oil, whisking until emulsified. Season with salt to taste. 

3. Place goat-cheese rounds on toasted bread slices, evenly covering each slice. Return baking sheet to oven’s top rack and bake until cheese browns in spots and melts slightly, 8-10 minutes. 

4. In a large bowl, toss radic-chio and/or frisée with herbs and radishes. Season with salt and pepper. Toss in enough dressing to lightly coat. Halve toasts and season with black pepper. Serve warm toasts alongside salad. Drizzle honey, if using, over toasts and salad. 

SLOW FOOD FAST / SATISFYING AND SEASONAL FOOD IN ABOUT 30 MINUTES 

LEAF SKILLS This salad will accommodate most any variety of chicory, though radicchio and frisée balance each other especially well. 

“IT CLICKED right away,” said Juan Cassal-ett of this salad, the first Slow Food Fast recipe from him and his wife, Jill Mathias. Simple as the recipe may be, the devel-opment process was anything but. In prep-aration for opening Chez Nous in Charles-ton, S.C., Mathias, the executive chef, and Cassalett, her right-hand at the time, de-veloped some 1,200 dishes. “It was nerve-racking!” Mathias confessed, but ulti-mately worth it. Over a decade later, this combination of chicories, herbs and warm goat-cheese toasts still features frequently on the daily changing menu at Chez Nous—particularly at this time of year, as we head into peak chicory season. “It’s best with a mix of frisée and radic-chio,” Mathias said of the chicory part of the equation. “The frisée is a bit sweet and ra-dicchio is bitter.” Sliced radishes and soft herbs provide texture and fragrance. The mustard dressing imparts an edge. The warm goat-cheese toasts make this salad a meal. To ensure a nice crunch, toast your sourdough slices before cover-ing them with thick rounds of chèvre. Then bake them until the cheese goes oozy, with a touch of browning. A final drizzle of honey is optional but highly rec-ommended. —Kitty Greenwald

What They're Known For Intimate restaurants serving appealingly rustic, considered classics. Nailing the timeless flavors of France, Italy and Spain. 

# IAM A JOURNALIST first and foremost,” said Toni Tipton-Martin. The editor in chief of Cook’s Country magazine, Tipton-Martin is also a leading authority on African American foodways and the author of award-winning books on the topic. Her latest, “Juke Joints, Jazz Clubs, and Juice: Cocktails from Two Centuries of African American Cookbooks” (Nov. 14, Clarkson Potter) focuses on the potable, bringing to light a fascinating history in recipes from Black bartenders and cooks. To immerse herself in the world of cocktails, this inveterate re-searcher relied on two “next-generation barmasters”: her son Brandon, who trained at several bars and restaurants, and Tiffanie Barriere, aka the Drinking Coach, a well-known bartender, educator and consultant. “My plan is to publish a series of single-subject books that highlights the various work of cooks in the African American community,” Tipton-Mar-tin said. Next up: a volume of desserts. In the lead-up to the hectic holi-day-entertaining season, she made time to welcome us into her Houston home for a Claret Cup cocktail and a chat at the bar. 

BY KARA NEWMAN 

AT MY BAR 

The bar tool I can’t live without is: 

a muddler. It’s theatrical. I can get out my fruits and spirits and talk to people while I’m muddling. It’s a very social instrument. There’s a section in the book about the West African tradition of macerating cit-rus fruit. The ancestors were mud-dling, drawing out the aromatics from the skin and the rinds, or pul-verizing the whole thing. Now it’s part of the historical story I get to tell while I’m making the drinks. 

The cocktail books I turn to again and again are: “The Ideal Bartender” by Tom Bullock, published in 1917, and “Julian’s Recipes” by Julian Anderson, published in 1919. They help us under-stand these authors’ fluency in cock-tail making, and the way they made these drinks their own. I like to stay very close to them in my creativity. If I need some modern help, then I take a peek at what [rapper] T-Pain has said in the book “Can I Mix You a Drink?” (2021). I get to see how the next gen-eration is interpreting ancestral ways. about it before I pour you a drink in it: my grandmother’s stemware, special gifts of cut crystal, beer steins my fa-ther brought home from Germany when my brother was stationed there. I went through a phase of col-lecting Depression-era glass, too. My grandmother had a whiskey decanter in the shape of Popeye. His pipe is broken after all these years, but he’s hanging in there. 

The best feature of my home drink-mixing setup is: the old-fash-ioned wet bar. We renovated it with a new backsplash but kept the origi-nal sink and faucet. 

The worst feature is: also the wet bar, for some people. They might think I should modernize the old fix-tures and the sink. But the fact that I can have a little corner of my house dedicated to mixology, and the storytelling I can do there, makes me happy. It doesn’t matter if it’s overly fancy. 

When I entertain, I like to: have a make-your-own cocktail bar. I might have a station with different fruits and syrups, and encourage people to adapt a drink according to their taste. Adapting isn’t appropriation unless you’re benefiting financially from my intellectual property. If we’re sharing ingredients and recipes and methods, that’s just normal recipe exchange. I like to have that discussion—where I got this recipe, how I changed it, how you can change it. 

When I’m traveling, I tend to drink: 

the same things I drink at home. Pi-not Noir. A Martini—I like someone else to mix it, and I ask for it dry. Be-fore creating the book I found it hard to order drinks out. I was still learning. Now that it’s completed, I’m eager to see how my interpreta-tions compare to others. 

A drink I love is: a Paloma, because grapefruit juice is not overly sweet. If a bartender is mixing it with gin-ger ale, I’ll ask for club soda or selt-zer instead, because I don’t want all that sugar. 

A drink trend I am over is: hibiscus tea. It’s a very popular beverage that appears on a lot of menus in cocktail form, particularly around the June-teenth holiday. I’ve certainly been a participant in that trend. But I’m more excited by the new ideas peo-ple are coming up with for celebrat-ing Juneteenth with the customary red drinks. We’ve all leaned on hibis-cus because it was there, and it had the historical connection [to West Africa]. But now I’m seeing more reclamation of watermelon; I’m see-ing berry drinks. Cups and punches are starting to emerge as people are talking about the ancestral mixolo-gists more. I’m excited about where red drinks are headed. 

Another obsession of mine apart from drinks is: baking. I love to bake. I bake when I’m happy, I bake when I’m sad. I bake when I’m distracted and am supposed to be writing. It’s my number-one procrastination tech-nique when I’m on deadline. 

—Adapted from an interview by Kara Newman 

My refrigerator is always stocked with: lemons and limes. A squeeze of citrus really balances out a cocktail. It provides another dimension, the way a pinch of salt can draw out something new in dishes. 

My bar is always stocked with: red wine, bubbles and whiskey. When I was president of the Southern Food-ways Alliance, friends there taught me to be a bourbon connoisseur. 

The most underrated ingredient is: 

cachaça, a Brazilian rum. I actually wouldn’t say underrated; I’d say up-and-coming. Other rums are made from molasses, a byproduct of sugar-cane processing; cachaça is made from fresh-pressed juice. It has a unique flavor that pairs with liqueurs you can float on top of your daiquiri. Talking about rum and African Ameri-can history is painful, as it relates to the Atlantic slave trade. This Brazilian style provides another conversation piece. 

On weekends, I typically mix: fruit-based drinks, anything I can muddle. I don’t typically drink on weeknights. It’s important to talk about modera-tion here. There’s a misrepresentation of African Americans as drunkards. In the book we included a section on zero-proof drinks to make sure people know you can celebrate with modera-tion, or without alcohol at all. 

The first thing people notice about my home bar is: the vintage glass-ware. I will be telling you stories 

> ARTURO OLMOS FOR THE WALL STREET JOURNAL

HER NAME IS MUDDLE Toni Tipton-Martin at home in Houston. Right, from top: Claret Cup in process; a stein from Germany; citrus, the great cocktail balancer. Inset: treasured references. 

Claret Cup 

Cups are wine-forward drinks often served in a pitcher or punch bowl. In-spired by recipes in “The Federation Cook Book” (1910) and “The American Waiter” (1914), this cup starts with claret, aka a red-wine blend; Cabernet Sauvignon or Merlot work well here, too. Cham-pagne and plenty of fruit make it festive. “Served in small tea cups, this cock-tail is good as a prelude to Thanksgiving dinner,” Tipton-Martin advised. 

Active Time 15 minutes 

Total Time 3 3

/4 hours (in-cludes macerating and chilling) Serves 30 

1 tablespoon granulated sugar 2 tablespoons warm water 3 oranges, sliced 2 lemons, sliced 1 pineapple, peeled, cored, and sliced into rings 1 ounce (2 tablespoons) Abricotine or apricot brandy 2 ounces ( 1

/4 cup) Curaçao 2 (750 ml) bottles claret of choice 2 (750 ml) bottles Champagne or other sparkling white wine 2 cups sparkling water 1 large block of ice 1 grapefruit, thinly sliced into half-moons 1. In a large bowl, com-bine sugar and warm wa-ter, stirring until sugar dissolves. Add orange, lemon and pineapple slices, and stir to thor-oughly coat fruit with syrup. Let fruit macerate, 30 minutes. 

2. Add Abricotine, Cura-çao and claret, and stir for 1 minute. Cover and re-frigerate for 3 hours. 

3. Strain mixture through a fine-mesh sieve, press-ing on pulp with a spoon to extract fruit juice. Pour into a punch bowl along with Champagne and sparkling water. Stir gently. Add ice block and garnish with grapefruit slices. To serve, ladle into Champagne flutes, punch cups or tea cups. 

—Adapted from ‘Juke Joints, Jazz Clubs, and Juice’ by Toni Tipton-Martin (Clarkson Potter) 

# Toni Tipton-Martin 

The award-winning author serves her festive drinks with a chaser of scintillating storytelling 

P2JW315000-4-D00800-1--------XA THE WALL STREET JOURNAL. * * * * Saturday/Sunday, November 11 - 12, 2023 | D9 

# IN SAN DIEGO, sunshine infuses homes so often—some 266 days a year, on average—that aggressively colorful interiors can easily come off as garish. What if you yearn for a fun, vivid look, but don’t want to live in the Mickey Mouse Clubhouse? That was the di-lemma facing a family that recently hired Mill Valley, Calif., designer Corine Maggio to re-vamp their 1935 Spanish Revival house in the city’s Point Loma neighborhood. Above all, the clients—an active couple with two high-school and college-age boys—craved a space that felt bold and hopeful, without a whiff of fussiness. “Aesthetically, they really wanted something happy,” Maggio explained. Adding to the challenge was an open-plan layout in which the living areas bled into each another—making a disciplined ap-proach to hue a must. To create colorful zones that played well together but never veered into “elementary school” vibes, Mag-gio took cues from European interiors that feature gutsy but slightly muted Old World hues. Happily, the mother of the family, who spends a lot of time in France, had tucked stacks of relevant reference materials into Maggio’s inspiration files. “The colors she was drawn to all had a relative softness to them,” Maggio said. The designer created another through-line by repeating the same hues—mostly shades of indigo, brick and ochre—so they ebb and flow throughout the space. For instance, the exact same blue-banded custom draperies make appearances in the office, sitting room and den, which unifies the spaces subtly without being boringly monotonous. “It ties the rooms together,” said Maggio. “But their personalities are still so distinct.” Here, the designer shares a look at the finished space, plus more strategies for cre-ating chic, kaleidoscopic rooms even grown-ups can love. 

BY KATHRYN O'SHEA-EVANS 

> LAURA RESEN, STYLED BY YEDDA MORRISON

How to achieve décor that’s not self-serious but doesn’t feel clownish? See this colorful Spanish Revival in San Diego. 

# Playing House 

SEEK EQUILIBRIUM 

At nearly 400 square feet, the scale of the family’s kitchen could easily overwhelm if too much color landed there. Maggio kept it feeling bright but bal-anced by confining emphatic color to one element: a clutter-concealing wall of powder-blue Shaker cabinets behind a white quartz island. Nearby, a simple back-splash of white zellige tile brings the volume down. A terrazzo floor rendered in shades of sand and seashells adds playfulness while an upholstered banquette and curli-cue bistro chairs layer on softness and Euro-pean charm. 

KNOW WHEN TO HOLD ’EM 

To provide breathing room in the central living area of a colorful, 1935 San Diego home, Mill Valley, Calif.-based interior designer Corine Maggio leaned on neutral elements. These include a geometric cof-fee table fashioned from stained banana bark, a tufted deep-green leather sofa and nubby, cream bouclé armchairs. “It’s a quiet respite from the other more en-ergized spaces,” the de-signer explained. Which isn’t to say that she en-tirely banished brighter colors. To make the space cohere with the rest of the home, she used artwork and candy-hued accessories to bring in the brave hues found in other rooms. A playful bench that spells out “amore” in lipstick red strikes a particularly droll, sculp-tural note. “It’s a total statement,” Maggio said—without taking up too much visual real estate. 

ANCHOR WITH ORGANICS 

In the den, where the family’s sons play videogames with pals, “tex-ture, texture, texture” served as the unifying theme. “The clients re-ally wanted this to be a place where everyone would want to hang out, so it had to be ultra cozy,” Maggio explained. A bulbous orange suede sofa might seem like a risky move, but the designer says its earthy undertones keep the look from skewing clownish. The other grounding elements: a pair of amoeboid braided abaca tables and a delicate pendant light fashioned from brass, fabric and rattan. On the ceiling, a canopy of palm-leaf wallpaper weaves in whimsy while making the space—already softened by a stain-resistant faux sheep-skin rug—even snugglier. While adventurous, the palette feels “rela-tively neutral” because the colors are rooted in nature, Maggio said. 

MAKE HISTORY 

In the primary suite, the clients’ request for a “cave-like” bedchamber gave Maggio license to embrace cocooning color. Anchoring the bunker: a scalloped bed upholstered top-to-bottom in a vintage-look botanical print from U.K. brand Clarke & Clarke. It echoes the palette down-stairs while amping up Old World drama. Above, an enveloping swath of imita-tion tin-tile wallpaper dis-covered at Home Depot crawls down the walls— and affordably evokes the layered look of an old European apartment, says Maggio, especially after it was painted a glossy bronze. 

DELIGHT IN DETAILS 

Even in a home with big spirit, small details can have surpris-ing impact. Consider the delicate floral wallpaper from Paris-based company Antoinette Poisson that Maggio’s client se-lected to line the jewel-box interior of the primary bath’s WC. “It’s so beautiful,” the designer said. “And the colors work well with the adjoining bedroom, so it was really serendipitous.” Outside, in a capacious walk-in shower, planes of classic hex tile rendered in sleek emerald zellige and soft white and black marble continue the hues without adding busyness. Nearby, a basket-weave light fixture commissioned from Maison Sarah Lavoine, a French company the homeowner adores, strikes a note of off-kilter charm. Said Maggio: “That was just a way to add something more fun.” 

A (RELATIVELY) QUIET PLACE 

In the living room, a neutral backdrop balances out bolder furnishings that weave in hues from surrounding rooms.

# #2 BEST ON THE WEST COAST 

Palisades Tahoe Ski Resort, Calif. 

Sierra Nevada snowfall blankets the all-you-can-ski buffet that is Palisades Tahoe, whose 6,000 skiable acres of steep runs, mellow glades and chill groomers are the best on the U.S. West Coast. Expansive options mean experts can swoop treeless bowls be-fore dropping into the pines, while begin-ners can play safely in areas designated for first timers. Contributing to that diversity: the 2011 addition of Alpine Meadows to the original Olympic Valley area where the 1960 Winter Games were held. A 2.4-mile, base-to-base gondola finally connected them last season, making it easier to ski the two areas in one day. Connoisseurs of the buttery-smooth late-season conditions known as “corn snow” revere Palisades Tahoe as one of the best places for spring skiing. The lifts generally keep whirring well into May, when pond-skimming events—in which skiers and snowboarders attempt to plane across a body of water—draw crowds. 

Walk-up day pass $199–279 ; 49 miles to Reno-Tahoe International Airport 

# #8 BEST FOR BEGINNERS 

Winter Park Resort, Colo. 

Newbies often fear they’ll blunder into a har-rowing descent that offers no safe escape. This resort’s abundance of easygoing terrain helps relieve those anxieties. Nearly a quarter of the runs at Colorado’s oldest continually operat-ing resort are designated for beginners. A ski and ride school in the resort’s Winter Park Territory (the mountain is sectioned into seven named zones called “territories”) offers packages for first timers that bundle rentals, lift tickets and half-day classes. Parents might appreciate that the school equips young stu-dents with GPS devices, which makes it easier for instructors to keep track of them and lets parents play-by-play each turn together with their kids while sharing cocoa and marshmal-lows after a day on the slopes. 

Walk-up day pass $69–259 ; 87 miles to Den-ver International Airport 

# #11 BEST FOR INTERMEDIATE SKIERS 

Heavenly Ski Resort, Calif./Nev. 

Not too easy, not too difficult: This South Lake Tahoe ski resort is the Goldilocks solu-tion for skiers seeking just-right difficulty levels in the Sierra Nevada. Intermediate trails account for the majority of runs across 4,800 skiable acres, and skiers look-ing to challenge themselves will find glades with plenty of room to plant turns between pines, hemlocks and firs. The high-speed North Bowl Express quad lift unveiled last season speeds access to the moderate Olympic Downhill run, which at 5.5 miles is

Luxury confers undeniable perks: The mountain has a rep for top-notch grooming, and daily caps on skier numbers have kept slopes uncrowded compared to nearby hills, even before a planned expan-sion that will more than double the resort’s skiable terrain, with 110 new runs due as soon as the 2025–2026 season. Deer Valley scores high on our après-ski ranking, too: Park kids with the on-mountain childcare and find adults-only respite (and craft cock-tails) at vintage-themed Royal Street Bar.

# #37 BEST ON THE EAST COAST 

Jay Peak Resort, Vt. 

None of the eastern resorts cracked our top 10—or even the top 30. Their numbers just can’t compete. But by wringing a lion’s share of precipitation from passing weather systems, border-hugging Jay Peak secures best-in-the-east status. Though the remote resort lacks the animated atmosphere of Vermont resorts like Stowe and Killington, Jay’s thrilling terrain courts true aficiona-dos. No over-grooming here: Despite a com-pact, 385-skiable-acre footprint, snow-trap-ping tree runs offer a sense of discovery. (Magnificent, at-your-own-risk backcountry expands the scope considerably.) Intermedi-ate skiers will find plenty to explore as well, including upper-mountain blue runs with views across the Green Mountains. Visitors can savor a cultural immersion as twangy Québécois accents abound on days when Canadians outnumber Americans. 

Attache ta tuque. 

Walk-up day pass $109–129, with at-par pricing for Canadians ; 66 miles to Patrick Leahy Burlington International Airport 

# #1 BEST OVERALL 

Whistler Blackcomb, British Columbia 

Sprawling across 8,171 skiable acres, the more than 200 marked runs at North Amer-ica’s biggest resort offer wraparound views of the jagged summits of British Columbia’s Fitzsimmons Range. Throw in annual snow-fall you could lose a giraffe in, nearly a mile of vertical drop and some of the best après in the biz, and it’s no surprise that dual-mountain Whistler Blackcomb Resort snags the top spot in our ranking. Choose-your-own-adventure terrain serves every level. Even tentative skiers can savor time above the tree line on Whistler’s scenic Burnt Stew Trail. Expert terrain ranges from 16 alpine bowls to steep chutes with iconic status; short, uphill hikes earn powdery descents on Shale Slope, Whistler Bowl and West Cirque. Those looking to up their technique can book private lessons with Olympic ath-letes, including Whistler local and gold med-alist Ashleigh McIvor. Sundays bring spec-tacular (and free) performances featuring pyrotechnics, music and world-class athletes soaring through giant flaming hoops. 

Walk-up day pass $157-$218 (C$215–299) ;84 miles to Vancouver International Airport 

# #16 BEST FOR FAMILIES 

Deer Valley Resort, Utah 

For families with generous budgets, this ritzy skier-only resort edges out the compe-tition with versatile terrain split equitably across difficulty levels, meaning you can carve up double-black glades while your

# #4 BEST FOR ADVANCED SKIERS 

Snowbird Ski Resort, Utah 

Vertiginous drops, steep moguls and spicy double blacks put experienced skiers to the test at Snowbird, a Wasatch Range rip-roarer where two-thirds of the runs are con-sidered advanced. Even the 1.6-mile tram up 11,000-foot Hidden Peak is bracing. Skiers look down at Great Scott, a famously diffi-cult run pinwheeling right beneath it at a 45-degree pitch. Fierce terrain is just the beginning: A lofty elevation means all the precipitation (436 inches, on average) mounds into featherweight fluff-piles and pillowy landings that are the stuff of pow-der-hound dreams. For next-level challenges, venture out of bounds with the experts. New at Snowbird this year are multiday packages from Powderbird, bundling guided Wasatch heli-skiing with rooms at the Cliff Lodge, spa time and resort access. 

Walk-up day pass $175–206 ; 29 miles to Salt Lake City International Airport 

# #5 BEST FOR BEER LOVERS 

Mt. Bachelor, Ore. 

Focusing too much on terrain and snowfall risks overlooking a ski-trip essential: post-trail beers. By cross-referencing more tradi-tional data with the number of surrounding breweries, we deduced that Oregon’s Mt. Bachelor is the best destination for anyone keen to make a snow-and-suds odyssey. The nearby Bend Ale Trail includes more than 30 brewery stops, from craft beer veterans like Deschutes Brewery to the eclectic Crux 

Continued from page D10 

Fermentation Project. Skiing holds its own amid the hops scene. Mt. Bachelor has 4,323 skiable acres and a marathon six-month season to enjoy the 368 inches of av-erage snowfall. If you can’t tear yourself from the mountain, rest assured that it, too, has beer. Debuting this year in a rustic cabin is the Boneyard Backcountry Bar, a collaboration with local brewery Boneyard Beer featuring outdoor seating and fire pits. 

Walk-up day pass $84–224 ; 38 miles to Red-mond Municipal Airport 

# #6 BEST IN COLORADO 

Aspen Snowmass, Colo. 

For many skiers, the Rocky Mountain State’s double-shot of feathery snow and craggy peaks remains the last word in winter bliss, and our best-in-Colorado resort delivers a 4-in-1 deal. Tickets to Aspen Snowmass grant access to Aspen Mountain, Aspen Highlands, Buttermilk and Snowmass, and it would take days to explore their combined 5,680 skiable acres. This year, a new high-speed quad lift at Aspen Mountain adds 153 acres of freshly accessible chutes and glades, including north-facing, high-altitude terrain where the resort’s 303-inch average annual snowfall lingers late in the season. Après-ski options are diversified, too: Chan-nel the spirit of local Hunter S. Thompson over burgers and whiskey at his old-timey former haunt J-Bar one day, and the next, join the fur-clad crowd for raclette, caviar and Champagne from French Alpine Bistro-Crêperie du Village. 

Walk-up day pass $203–293 ; 2 miles to As-pen/Pitkin County Airport, 220 miles to Denver International Airport 

# #30 BEST FOR HOT-TUBBERS 

Sun Valley Resort, Idaho 

If you’re seeking the steamy epicenter of American hot-tub culture, consider Sun Val-ley, Idaho, where a love for convivial poaching runs deep. Our analysis of 2023 real estate listings in Sun Valley found that 73% included hot tubs. That surfeit boosts your odds of scoring a rental with a private tub, but, for dedicated soakers, Sun Valley, with its sim-mering geothermal activity, offers wilder bounty, too. If you have a sense of adventure (and, preferably, a four-wheel-drive vehicle), seek out Sawtooth National Forest’s French-man’s Bend Hot Springs. In between soak ses-sions, pencil in time at the resort’s two ski ar-eas: beginner-friendly Dollar Mountain, and, for the more ambitious, Bald Mountain, which offers spectacular bowls and an impressive 3,400-foot vertical. 

Walk-up day pass at Bald Mountain

THE BEST RESORTS FOR MULTI-MOUNTAIN PASS HOLDERS 

As walk-up lift tickets push $300, even occasional skiers can wring value from Ikon or Epic Passes, the two multi-mountain passes that now dominate the industry and grant access to dozens of participating mountains around the world. Procrastinators be warned: Booking before fast-approaching seasonal cutoff dates is a must. Wondering which one to choose? Below are our top-ranked North American mountains for each. 

Multi-pass math Ikon Passes with no blackout dates are currently $1,309, subject to change, and are sold through mid-December. Epic Passes with no blackout dates are $969 through Nov. 18, when prices rise; pass sales generally end in late November or early December.

#5 

Some travelers insist ski resorts’ best en-ticements lie beyond the slopes. “I’m all about après ski without the skiing,” said Jill Robbins, 57, of San Antonio, who re-nounced the sport midway through a di-sastrous first lesson at Colorado’s Key-stone Resort. “I like winter weather and snow and the idea of drinking hot buttered rum and hot chocolate by a roaring fire.” “I adore the ski-culture vibe,” agreed Heide Brandes, 50, of Oklahoma City. “I never quite learned how to ski, but I do love snowshoe hikes, cross-country stuff, goofy snow tubing.” Brandes suggests like-minded travelers check out the low-key Angel Fire Resort in New Mexico, with its two-lane tubing hill and a Nordic center where snowshoers and cross-coun-try skiers can explore 7.5 miles of trails. Not every winter resort is suited to non-skiers, said Anthony Luczkow, a mountain travel expert for travel agency Ski.com, who encourages clients to con-sider towns with off-mountain amenities. “Aspen is always a good go-to because it’s going to offer easy access to activities, great dining and shopping,” Luczkow said. With its base villages, fat biking, snow-shoeing and slope-side spas, Vail fits the bill for skiers and non-skiers alike, too. Those seeking more adventurous trips can book wilderness-adjacent spots such as Jackson Hole Mountain Resort in Teton Vil-lage, Wyo. “It still has great dining and great shopping, but what it really offers is access to Yellowstone National Park and Grand Teton National Park,” Luczkow said. Snow-drifted roads mean uncrowded parks, and visitors on snowmobile tours or in en-closed “snowcoaches” can more easily spot wildlife like wolves and bison. Follow snowy sessions of watching wildlife and geysers with whiskey and country tunes at legend-ary Teton Village bar the Mangy Moose— proof that for an ideal après experience, there really is no skiing required. 

For many, the real appeal of a ‘ski weekend’ is everything that happens off the slopes.

# GEAR & GADGETS 

The Wall Street Journal is not compensated by retailers listed in its articles as outlets for products. Listed retailers frequently are not the sole retail outlets. 

SCREEN TIME 

Amy Chen, 28, a Brooklyn-based brand consultant, has been sewing on the same Brother machine since high school. Yet many of its fea-tures still stump her. She de-scribes its user interface as “so bad…like trying to decipher ancient Egyptian.” The up-dated version of Chen’s ma-chine, the Brother CS7000X, solves many of these prob-lems by adding a highly legi-ble LCD screen; its intuitive, uncluttered interface lets you use all the machine’s features, like 70 built-in stitch types, more easily. And without be-ing fluent in hieroglyphs. 

HEAVY METAL 

Those who plan to work with technical or heavyweight fab-rics should find a sewing ma-chine with a metal body, says Meredith. Unfortunately, these often-beastly models can be too hulking for apartments or small starter homes. An ex-ception: Singer’s latest heavy-duty domestic machine, the aptly named Heavy Duty 4452. Though only 15.5-inches wide, it combines a metal chassis with a motor 50% more pow-erful than that of a typical home machine, and can stitch through thick fabrics without breaking a sweat. Its diamond-tipped needles can even sew through leather, a big flex. 

YOUTUBE U 

Singh jokes that he sharp-ened his sewing skills where most millennials learn most things: “the university of You-Tube.” Sewing machine com-panies got the memo—many release video tutorials on so-cial media channels and ac-companying apps, detailing how to get the most out of specific machines. The New Home Janome Arctic Crystal, for example, comes with a YouTube series that walks you through its many func-tions, including its four ad-justable presser feet for snag-free sewing. The free tutorials are like a full season of Proj-ect Runway, without the irk-some reminder that you just need to “make it work.” 

—Molly Collett 

IN STITCHES High-tech, app-enabled machines have young Americans making their own clothes 

FOUNDED IN 1959, American Honda Motor Company moved into its current headquarters in Torrance, Calif., in 1990. But it took until last month for them to fix up the lobby. The atrium-style space now displays some of Honda’s historical collection: game-changing cars, such as the Accord and Civic; life-changing motorcycles (CB 750, Gold Wing); outboard motors, lawn mowers and the tiniest, most adorable gas-powered gen-erators evah , designed to run itty-bitty ’60s-era TVs. Suffice to say, Honda knows cute when it sees it. So when an employee in Torrance presented managers with his after-hours passion project—a foldable battery-powered micro-scooter with a form factor like a SIM card—it was approved for production “instantly,” said Honda spokesperson Andrew Quillin. Thus was born the Honda Motocompacto ($995, as tested), a last-mile micro-mobility solution light enough to lift and carry (42 pounds) or merrily roll along beside you, on or off the train. Convenient to store beside or under a desk, the Motocompacto has a built-in charger that allows its wee battery to be replenished on household current in 3.5 hours, for a nominal range of 12 miles. and it returned the favor. The front-wheel integrated motor puts out a whopping 0.7-hp, so stand back, children. Top speed is 15 mph, officially, but I could only get it to 14 mph. I asked the Honda rep about the dis-crepancy and he gave me an up-and-down look. Oh, right. I might have covered 3 miles, with the thumb-actuated throttle pinned. I don’t rec-ommend curb-jumping. The only elasticity in the chassis comes from the solid rubber tires and the allegedly rubberized seat. The remaining vertical loads travel undamped up the spine to the rider’s clenched molars. Still, it’s light, quiet and nimble, effort-less to slalom around bollards on pedestrian plazas—or pedestrians, for that matter. For a moment I saw myself from the out-side, laughing madly and weaving between bemused Honda employees. Oh dear. It’s fi-nally happened: I’m eccentric. I began to doubt my own recognizance. And yet I also observed that the madcap Motocompacto was making people happy on sight. Engineered in Ohio and built in China, the Motocompacto—even the name is hilari-ous—is designed to negotiate the liminal ur-ban spaces of sidewalks, plazas and bike lanes, cohabitating with bikes and pedestri-ans. An LED headlight, positioned about 16 inches above the pavement, is less for see-ing than for being seen. It has a bell. It is most emphatically not street-legal. Lordy. I can’t even imagine. And yet in many ways the Motocompacto is self-evidently superior to conventional ur-ban scooter upon which literally millions of young people rely every day. First, it’s safer to sit than stand. Its compactification makes it convenient to carry, and therefore to own, not rent by the mile. The wheels are retract-able; the handlebars and kickstand disap-pear between the polycarbonate panels, along with the detachable saddle. The kick-stand has a small welded loop to secure it with a chain or cable. The folded product has a comfortable soft-plastic handle on a webbed strap, as any good card table would. The Motocompacto name recalls a folding micro-scooter of the 1980s, the two-stroke Motocompo, which was sold as an accessory to certain Honda cars, stored in the trunk. Oh man, I bet that reeked. What is that be-guiling scent you’re wearing, darling? Advantage: electric. Honda—the global giant and its American subsidiary—has been slow to embrace elec-trification. The Motocompacto coincides with the launch of the company’s first mod-ern EV automobiles, the 2024 Honda Pro-logue and Acura ZDX, which share GM’s Ul-tium platform. Honda has said it will bring 30 new EV products to the market by 2030, with global sales volumes of 2 million units. It calls the Motocompacto a “fun and inno-vative facet of the larger Honda electrifica-tion strategy.” Behold, the littlest brand icon. Postscript: Some readers noted that my recent review of Michael Mann’s “Ferrari” neglected to list “Ford vs. Ferrari” (2019) among the better racing films. It wasn’t en-tirely an oversight. As a lifelong student of automotive history, I knew this chapter a lit-tle too well. Indeed, many of the tales felt too oft told and some characters—Matt Da-mon’s Carroll Shelby—etched in hero wor-ship. I’d had a couple of professional en-counters with Mr. Shelby over the years. He was more interesting than that. 

RUMBLE SEAT / DAN NEIL 

# The Petite Charm of an Electric Micro-Scooter 

> HONDA

SMALL WONDER The Honda Motocompacto is a lightweight, compact, foldable electric scooter. 

THIS SEPTEMBER , Colin Mer-edith, a 29-year-old in Vancou-ver, Canada, went on a six-day camping trip with his brother. Surviving a week in the Cana-dian wilderness, especially with a sibling, calls for forti-tude. More impressive: Mere-dith, a freelance clothing de-signer with clients like Rapha, made his tent, sleeping bag and clothes on his home sew-ing machine. Sewing, as a hobby, evokes grandmas more than campers. Indeed, many learn the basics from an older relative. At some point, though, a lot of would-be tailors discover that old-school models leave room for im-provement. When he started sewing this year, Am-ardeep Singh, 32, got away with us-ing just such a family machine. But when the Brooklyn, N.Y.-based UX designer tried feed-ing it trendy technical fabrics, it balked. “It was just freaking out. Broken needles. Chaos,” he said. Upgrading to a newer Singer machine that can han-dle ripstop nylon solved the issue. Bonus: It comes with an app that aids setup. Such features have made sewing machines more friendly to the young, who of-ten post their projects on so-cial media sites like Instagram and TikTok. Meredith says generational disillusionment with mass con-sumption and fast fashion also fuels the trend. After all, you would be hard pressed to find a slower way to get clothes. Below, three more ad-vantages newer machines offer next-gen sewers. 

How the advantages of next-gen sewing machines are wooing the young 

> SOL COTTI

# Respond to the Thread 

Singer Heavy Duty 4452 Sewing Machine, $310, Singer.com 

What’s so instantly right about the Mo-tocompacto design? Like the shoebox-sized generators in Honda’s gallery, the Motocom-pacto has the ineffable charm of a small ma-chine (½-hp) assigned a big job—a Mighty Mite. Also, in its softly futuristic, modular design, I spy a certain cosplay effect: This is what Imperial stormtrooper cadets would ride to calculus class. Now on sale, the Motocompacto is only available in NASA white—that is to say, with literal white space to be filled in, deco-rated or rented out as the owner likes. I was invited to think of the way kids person-alize their laptops and phones. And yet it looks pretty compelling as is. One day hu-mans may ride these on Mars. They too will look goofy. Wearing a tailored suit and a polo-style helmet, I gave the Motocompacto a good thrashing around Honda’s campus last week, 

I observed that the madcap Motocompacto was making people happy on sight.
